Email address: jiajie.chen@cims.nyu.edu , Giorgio Cialdea Address: Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences, New York University, New York, NY 10012. Email address: giorgio.cialdea@cims.nyu.edu , Steve Shkoller Address: Department of Mathematics, University of California Davis, Davis, CA 95616. Email address: shkoller@math.ucdavis.edu and Vlad Vicol Address: Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences, New York University, New York, NY 10012. Email address: vicol@cims.nyu.edu Abstract. We prove finite-time vorticity blowup for smooth solutions of the 2D compressible Euler equations with smooth, localized, and non-vacuous initial
data. The vorticity blowup occurs at the time of the first singularity, and is accompanied by an axisymmetric implosion in which the swirl velocity enjoys full stability, as opposed to finite co-dimension stability. 1. Introduction We consider the isentropic compressible Euler equations in multiple space dimensions (1.1a) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+\operatorname{div}(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})+\nabla p$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ (1.1b) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\operatorname{div}(\rho\mathbf{u})$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ where $\mathbf{u}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the velocity vector field and $\rho\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}$ is the strictly positive density function. The pressure $p\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}$ is determined from the density via the ideal gas law (1.1c) $p=\tfrac{1}{\gamma}\rho^{\gamma},$ where $\gamma>1$ is the adiabatic exponent.
We supplement the Euler equations with initial data (1.1d) $(\mathbf{u},\rho)|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{u}_{0},\rho_{0}),$ which is as nice as it gets . By this, we mean that $(\mathbf{u}_{0},\rho_{0})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ , $\rho_{0}\geq{\rm constant}>0$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ , and $(|\mathbf{u}_{0}(x)|,\rho_{0}(x))\to(0,{\rm constant})$ as $|x|\to\infty$ , at a sufficiently fast rate; here, $d\geq 2$ is the space dimension. With these assumptions on $(\mathbf{u}_{0},\rho_{0})$ , the classical well-posedness theory [ 39 , 52 , 50 ] guarantees the existence of a maximal time $T>0$ , and of a unique solution $(\mathbf{u},\rho)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,T))$ of the Cauchy problem for the Euler equations ( 1.1 ). Generically, we expect that $T<\infty$ , a singularity forms in finite time [ 53 , 49 , 67 ] . While it is well-known that at the time $T$ of the first singularity the solution must develop infinite gradients, i.e. $\limsup_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}\|(\nabla\mathbf{u},\nabla\rho)(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\infty$ , a complete classification of all possible singularity mechanisms attainable by smooth Euler solutions $(\mathbf{u},\rho)(\cdot,t)$ as $t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T<\infty$ remains, to date, elusive. For instance, while it is known that a pre-shock singularity [ 27 , 29 , 8 , 6 , 9 , 10 , 55 , 56 ] or an implosion singularity [ 62 , 63 , 11 , 5 ] may form as $t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T$ from smooth initial conditions, it is an outstanding open problem to determine whether $\limsup_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}\|\omega(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\infty$ , where $\omega=\nabla\times\mathbf{u}$ denotes the fluid vorticity. In the case that the Euler solution evolves to a stable gradient blowup at a time $T<\infty$ , from generic , compressive, and smooth initial data, the vorticity $\omega(\cdot,t)$ remains uniformly bounded in $C^{1/3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for $t\in[0,T]$ , as was shown in [ 8 , 6 , 9 , 10 , 66 ] . The fact that the vorticity does not blow up at the time of the first
gradient singularity 1 1 1 The spacetime point at which the first gradient singularity forms is the point on the
spacetime co-dimension- $2$ manifold of pre-shocks, with a minimal time coordinate (see [ 66 ] ). should not come as a surprise for $d=2$ , since the specific vorticity $\omega/\rho$ is transported by the fluid velocity: (1.2) $\partial_{t}\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)=0\,.$ Since the density in [ 8 , 6 , 9 , 10 , 66 ] remains bounded at the time of the first gradient blowup,
and the initial datum is non-vacuous, it follows from the maximum principle for ( 1.2 ) that $\|\omega(\cdot,T)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty$ . For $d=3$ , the proof of this upper bound for the maximum vorticity is more subtle (see [ 10 ] ). The specific vorticity transport equation ( 1.2 ) shows that for $d=2$ the vorticity can blow up at time $T$ only if an implosion singularity simultaneously develops at time $T$ , i.e. $\lim_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}\|\rho(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}$ . This, however, need not be an if and only if statement. Indeed, the smooth implosions established in [ 62 , 63 , 5 ] are confined to radially symmetric solutions, and hence $\omega(\cdot,t)\equiv 0$ for all $t\in[0,T]$ . The only rigorous proof of a non-radially symmetric implosion from smooth initial data was recently established in [ 11 ] for $d=3$ . Such non-radial imploding solutions possess non-trivial vorticity, but establishing vorticity blowup is incongruous with the finite co-dimension stability framework employed in [ 11 ] . In particular, since vorticity waves propagate along fluid-velocity characteristics 2 2 2 The specific vorticity $\omega/\rho$ is transported when $d=2$ (see ( 1.2 )) and is Lie-advected when $d=3$ (see ( 2.9 )). , there exists a distinguished initial fluid particle $x_{*}$ such that $\omega_{0}(x_{*})$ is “carried to” the implosion along the fluid-velocity characteristics, thereby determining the vorticity $\omega(\cdot,T)$ at the point of the implosion. As the stability analysis in [ 11 ] requires $\omega_{0}$ to be a finite co-dimension perturbation of the zero function, and does not specify the precise subspace associated to this finite co-dimension, it is not possible to guarantee that $\omega_{0}(x_{*})\neq 0$ (which must be ensured in order to establish vorticity blowup). We shall explain this issue in greater detail in Section 2.7 below. The goal of this paper is to give the first proof of finite-time blowup for the vorticity in the compressible Euler equations, with smooth initial data — see Theorem 1.1 below. 1.1. The two-dimensional axisymmetric ansatz In order to state our main result, it is convenient to first introduce a symmetric form of the isentropic Euler system ( 1.1 ), in which the fundamental variables are the fluid velocity $\mathbf{u}$ and the rescaled sound speed $\sigma$ , which is defined by $\sigma=\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\sqrt{\tfrac{dp}{d\rho}}=\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha},\quad\alpha=\tfrac{\gamma-1}{2}.$ In terms of the unknowns $\mathbf{u}$ and $\sigma$ the Euler equations ( 1.1 ) are equivalent to the following system 3 3 3 In the full Euler system the specific entropy is transported along the flow of the vector field $(\partial_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)$ . As such, for isentropic initial data (i.e., with constant specific entropy at time $t=0$ ) the solution will remain isentropic up to the time $T$ of the very first singularity, which justifies the usage of the ( 1.1 ) and hence of ( 1.3 ) on $[0,T]$ . : (1.3a) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}+\alpha\,\sigma\,\nabla\sigma$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ (1.3b) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\sigma+\alpha\,\sigma\,\mathrm{div}\mathbf{u}$ $\displaystyle=0\,.$ For the remainder of the paper, we restrict our analysis to the two-dimensional case : $d=2$ . As such, it is convenient to rewrite ( 1.3 ) in
polar coordinates $(R,\theta)$ , defined as $R=|x|,\quad\theta=\arctan\bigl(\tfrac{x_{2}}{x_{1}}\bigr),\quad\mathbf{e}_{R}=(\cos\theta,\text{sin }\theta),\quad\mathbf{e}_{\theta}=(-\text{sin }\theta,\cos\theta).$ Throughout the paper, we shall use boldface font $\mathbf{u},\mathbf{U},\ldots$ to denote vectors,
and standard unbolded font $u,U,\ldots$ to denote scalars. We analyze vorticity blowup within the class of two-dimensional axisymmetric flows , a class of flows with built-in rotation. That is, we consider a velocity field $\mathbf{u}$ with the following axisymmetry 4 4 4 If $u^{\theta}\equiv 0$ in ( 1.4 ), we recover radially symmetric functions $(\mathbf{u},\sigma)$ . : (1.4) $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(R,\theta,t)=\mathbf{u}^{R}+\mathbf{u}^{\theta},\quad\mathbf{u}^{R}=u^{R}(R,t)\mathbf{e}_{R},\quad\mathbf{u}^{\theta}=u^{\theta}(R,t)\mathbf{e}_{\theta},\quad\sigma=\sigma(R,t).$ The vorticity and velocity-divergence associated to the axisymmetric ansatz ( 1.4 ) are given by $\displaystyle\omega$ $\displaystyle=\omega(R,t)=\bigl(\tfrac{1}{R}+\partial_{R}\bigr)u^{\theta}(R,t),$ $\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}$ $\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}^{R}(R,t)=\bigl(\tfrac{1}{R}+\partial_{R}\bigr)u^{R}(R,t).$ In general, for a smooth function $f=f(|x|)\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}$ we have $\operatorname{div}(f\mathbf{e}_{R})=R^{-1}\partial_{R}(Rf(R))$ ; as such, we shall often abuse notation and simply write $\operatorname{div}(f(R))=R^{-1}\partial_{R}(Rf(R))$ in place of $\operatorname{div}(f\mathbf{e}_{R})$ . In order to show that the two-dimensional axisymmetric ansatz ( 1.4 ) is preserved by the Euler evolution, we note that the system ( 1.3 ) is equivalent (for smooth solutions) to the system ( 1.7 ) below, which provides evolution equations for $(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma)=(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma)(R,t)$ . For this purpose, it is convenient to recall the following identities, which hold for smooth functions $f=f(R)$ : (1.5) $\mathbf{u}^{R}\cdot\nabla f=u^{R}\partial_{R}f,\quad u^{\theta}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\cdot\nabla(f\mathbf{e}_{R})=\tfrac{1}{R}u^{\theta}f\mathbf{e}_{\theta},\quad u^{\theta}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\cdot\nabla(f\mathbf{e}_{\theta})=-\tfrac{1}{R}u^{\theta}f\mathbf{e}_{R}.$ Using the ansatz ( 1.4 ) and the identities in ( 1.5 ), we may decompose the system ( 1.3 ) into radial and angular parts, leading to the equivalent formulation (1.6a) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}^{R}+\mathbf{u}^{R}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{R}+\mathbf{u}^{\theta}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\theta}+\alpha\,\sigma\,\nabla\sigma$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ (1.6b) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}^{\theta}+\mathbf{u}^{R}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\theta}+\mathbf{u}^{\theta}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{R}$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ (1.6c) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\mathbf{u}^{R}\cdot\nabla\sigma+\alpha\,\sigma\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}^{R}$ $\displaystyle=0\,.$ In terms of the scalar quantities $(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma)=(\mathbf{u}^{R}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{R},\mathbf{u}^{\theta}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{\theta},\sigma)$ , the system ( 1.6 ) may be written as (1.7a) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{R}+u^{R}\partial_{R}u^{R}+\alpha\sigma\partial_{R}\sigma$ $\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{R}(u_{\theta})^{2}\,,$ (1.7b) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{\theta}+u^{R}\partial_{R}u^{\theta}+\tfrac{1}{R}u^{\theta}u^{R}$ $\displaystyle=0\,,$ (1.7c) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+u^{R}\partial_{R}\sigma+\alpha\,\sigma\operatorname{div}(u^{R})$ $\displaystyle=0\,.$ Both reformulations of the isentropic Euler system for two-dimensional axisymmetric flows, i.e. systems ( 1.6 ) and ( 1.7 ), when transformed to self-similar variables and coordinates (see ( 2.1 ) below), play important roles in the subsequent
analysis. The self-similar version of ( 1.7 ) is used to study blowup profiles, while the self-similar version of ( 1.6 ) is used for
energy estimates and the majority of bounds on the solution. 1.2. The main result As mentioned in the discussion following ( 1.2 ), in two space dimensions the vorticity can blow up at time $T$ only if an implosion singularity simultaneously develops at time $T$ . Since we seek smooth Euler solutions on $[0,T)$ , we aim to
construct a stable perturbation (for the angular velocity) of
a smoothly imploding self-similar solution , as was recently constructed in [ 62 ] , and further refined in [ 5 ] . The self-similarity employed in [ 62 ] , [ 5 ] , and herein, is of the second kind.
A similarity exponent $r>1$ is determined as the solution of a nonlinear eigenvalue problem, and is then used to define the
self-similar space $\xi=\frac{R}{(T-t)^{1/r}}$ and time $s=-\frac{1}{r}\log(T-t)$ coordinates, together with self-similar state variables $(U,A,\Sigma)(\xi,s)=r(T-t)^{(r-1)/r}(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma)(R,t)$ ; see ( 2.1 ) below. The self-similar variables $(U,A,\Sigma)$ solve the self-similar version of the Euler system; see ( 2.3 ) below.
Of particular interest for our analysis are special $C^{\infty}$ -smooth stationary solutions $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ to this self-similar system; we refer to the triplet $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ as the self-similar blowup profile . We choose to work directly with the smooth imploding self-similar profiles $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ from [ 62 , Theorem 1.2, Corollary 1.3] , as these profiles, together with their repulsive properties (see ( 2.4b ) below), are readily available for $d=2$ . 5 5 5 While it is expected that the arguments in [ 5 ] also apply to the case that $d=2$ (used in this paper), [ 5 ] only considers $d=3$ . The slight drawback of using the results from [ 62 ] is that we need to exclude an exceptional countable sequence of adiabatic exponents $\Gamma=\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}$ , which is possibly empty, and whose accumulation points can only be $\{1,2,\infty\}$ . For each $\gamma\not\in\Gamma$ , it is shown in [ 62 ] (and recalled in Theorem 2.1 below) that there exists a discrete sequence of admissible blowup speeds $\{r_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset(1,r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha))$ , accumulating at the value $r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha)$ defined in ( 2.2 ), such that the Euler equations ( 1.7 ) exhibit smooth, radially symmetric (meaning
that $u^{\theta},A\equiv 0$ ), globally self-similar, imploding solutions with similarity exponent $r_{k}$ and profile $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ (see Section 2.1 below). With this notation fixed, we state our main result. Theorem 1.1 ( Main result). Fix a non-exceptional adiabatic exponent $\gamma\not\in\Gamma$ , let $\alpha=(\gamma-1)/2$ , and let $r>1$ be an admissible blowup speed satisfying ( 2.2 ), as in [ 62 ] . Then, there exists initial data $(u_{0}^{R},u_{0}^{\theta})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ and $\sigma_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ with $\sigma_{0}\geq{\rm constant}>0$ , 6 6 6 The initial sound speed $\sigma_{0}$ may be chosen to equal a constant outside a compact set in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ ; see Section 4 . such that the solution $(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma)$ of ( 1.7 ) exhibits a finite time singularity at a time $T<\infty$ . The radial velocity $u^{R}$ , the sound speed $\sigma$ , and the vorticity $\omega$ all blow up at time $T$ . The blowup is asymptotically self-similar in the sense that (1.8a) $\displaystyle\lim_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}r\big(T-t\big)^{\!\!{\frac{r-1}{r}}}u^{R}((T-t)^{\frac{1}{r}}\xi,t)$ $\displaystyle=\bar{U}(\xi),$ (1.8b) $\displaystyle\lim_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}r\big(T-t\big)^{\!\!{\frac{r-1}{r}}}u^{\theta}((T-t)^{\frac{1}{r}}\xi,t)$ $\displaystyle=0,$ (1.8c) $\displaystyle\lim_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}r\big(T-t\big)^{\!\!{\frac{r-1}{r}}}\sigma((T-t)^{\frac{1}{r}}\xi,t)$ $\displaystyle=\bar{\Sigma}(\xi),$ for $\xi\in(0,\infty)$ , where $\bar{U}$ and $\bar{\Sigma}$ are the similarity profiles from [ 62 ] . The vorticity blows up at $R=0$ as (1.8d) $\lim_{t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T}\big(T-t\big)^{\!\!{\frac{r-1}{\alpha r}}}\omega(0,t)=\omega_{0}(0)\left(\tfrac{\bar{\Sigma}(0)}{r\sigma_{0}(0)}\right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{\alpha}}\neq 0,$ and there are no singularities away from the point of implosion: $(R,t)=(0,T)$ . An outline of the proof of Theorem 1.1 is given in Section 2 . Before turning to the proof, we make a few comments concerning the statement of Theorem 1.1 . Remark 1.2 ( The set of initial data for vorticity blowup). As explained in Remark 4.2 below, there exists an open set $X_{2}$ (a ball in a weighted Sobolev space) and a finite co-dimension set $X_{1}$ such that the initial data in Theorem 1.1 may be taken such that $(\mathbf{u}^{R}_{0},\sigma_{0})\in X_{1}$ and $\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\theta}\in X_{2}$ . It is the fact that $\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\theta}$ belongs to an open set which allows us to ensure that the initial vorticity $\omega_{0}$ does not vanish at $R=0$ . Remark 1.3 ( The curl blows up slower than the divergence). Since $\omega_{0}(0)\neq 0$ , identity ( 1.8d ) shows that the vorticity blows up at $R=0$ at the rate $(T-t)^{-\frac{r-1}{\alpha r}}$ .
Moreover, since the admissible blowup speed $r$ satisfies the constraint ( 2.2 ), it follows that $0<\frac{r-1}{\alpha\,r}<\frac{r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha)-1}{\alpha\,r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha)}<1$ for all $\alpha>0$ , and therefore $\int_{0}^{T}\|\omega(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}dt\approx\int_{0}^{T}|\omega(0,t)|dt<+\infty$ . As the magnitude of vorticity is integrable in time, there does not exist a Beale-Kato-Majda type blowup criterium for the compressible Euler equations.
As to the effects of compression, from ( 1.8a ) and ( 2.4e ), we have that $|\!\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}(0,t)|\approx(T-t)^{-1}(2/r)|\bar{U}^{\prime}(0)|=(T-t)^{-1}(r-1)/(\alpha r)$ , which implies
that $\int_{0}^{T}\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}dt=+\infty$ . The fact that $\operatorname{div}u$ blows up faster than $\omega$ indicates that sound waves steepen in compression much faster than particles can “swirl”. Remark 1.4 ( There are no singularities away from $R=0$ and $t=T$ ). While Theorem 1.1 shows that the rescaled sound speed $\sigma(0,t)$ and the magnitude of the gradients $|\nabla\mathbf{u}|(0,t)$ and $|\nabla\sigma|(0,t)$ blow up as $t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T$ , we emphasize that, by construction, the Euler solution in Theorem 1.1 does not form singularities for times $t<T$ , and moreover, as $t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T$ no singularities develop at points $x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}$ with $x\neq 0$ . Indeed, for $x\neq 0$ and $t\in[0,T)$ arbitrary, upper bounds for $|\mathbf{u}(x,t)|$ are obtained in ( 4.47a ), upper and lower bounds bounds for $\sigma(x,t)$ are given by ( 4.48 ), while upper bounds for $|\nabla\mathbf{u}(x,t)|$ and $|\nabla\sigma(x,t)|$ are established in ( 4.47b ). We refer to Section 4.5 for these details. 1.3. Recent results concerning singularity formation for Euler The literature concerning the formation and propagation of singularities for the compressible Euler equations (and related hyperbolic PDEs) is too vast
to review here. 7 7 7 In contrast, relatively few results have been established for the finite time singularity formation of the incompressible Euler equations; for instance, finite-time vorticity blowup remains open for the incompressible Euler equations posed on ${\mathbb{R}}^{d}$ or ${\mathbb{T}}^{d}$ , for $C_{c}^{\infty}$ , respectively $C^{\infty}$ , initial conditions.
We note, however, that very recently, the finite time blowup for the vorticity was established in the presence of smooth solid boundaries [ 22 , 23 ] , for the equations posed in conical domains [ 35 ] , or in the absence of boundaries for $C^{1,\alpha}$ data with $\alpha$ very small [ 33 , 21 , 34 , 36 , 30 ] . In this section, we shall focus only on results for the Euler equations , 8 8 8 There is a large literature concerning singularity formation for quasilinear wave equations; see the recent review articles [ 43 , 68 ] . and review only those results for the multi-dimensional problem . 9 9 9 For a detailed exposition of the 1D theory we refer the reader to [ 16 , 31 , 54 ] and references therein. As noted above, it is well-known that the Euler equations develop finite-time gradient singularities from initial data which is as nice as it gets . The first proof of this fact was given in [ 67 ] , using a proof by contradiction , inspired by the classical results [ 53 , 49 ] . In recent decades, authors have given constructive proofs of singularity formation, which yield a very precise description of the solution at the time of the first gradient blowup. We categorize these constructive proofs into shock formation and smooth implosions . 1.3.1. Shock formation Shock formation from smooth and localized initial conditions is, by now, well understood, up to the time of the first gradient singularity . In the context of irrotational solutions for the multi-dimensional Euler equations, blowup results based on ideas developed for second-order
quasilinear wave equations and general relativity were established in [ 3 , 2 , 27 , 29 ] ; the latter two results were generalized to allow for vorticity and entropy in the Euler solution [ 55 , 56 ] ; results for 2D Euler solutions, with azimuthal symmetry, using modulated self-similar analysis were proven in [ 8 , 7 ] ; results for 3D Euler solutions in the absence of symmetry assumptions, based on modulated self-similar stability analysis were established in [ 9 , 10 ] .
In the context of shock development, results for Euler solutions with spherical symmetry were obtained in [ 72 , 28 ] , while results for
Euler flows with azimuthal symmetry were proven in [ 6 ] . Very recently, shock formation past the time of the first singularity was analyzed in [ 66 ] and [ 1 ] . In [ 66 ] the last two authors of this paper analyze the evolution of fast-acoustic characteristic surfaces and use this smooth geometric information to describe the Euler solution up to a portion of the boundary of the maximal globally hyperbolic development (MGHD) of smooth Cauchy data, which includes both the
singular set as well as the Cauchy horizon; the results of [ 1 ] evolve the Euler solution up to a portion of the boundary of the MGHD, containing only the
singular set. 1.3.2. Smooth Implosions The classical Guderley problem [ 42 ] deals with the implosion of a converging shock-wave, and the continuation as an expanding shock. In spite of the vast literature on this subject, the existence of a finite-time smooth implosion (without a shock already present in the solution at the initial time), was established only recently in [ 62 , 63 ] , and further refined in [ 11 , 5 ] . While these results are inspired by the ansatz and general strategy in [ 42 ] , the existence of $C^{\infty}$ self-similar implosion profiles requires a great deal of mathematical sophistication. Moreover, the stability of these solutions can only be established for finite-co-dimension perturbations which are not precisely quantified (see the numerical work [ 4 ] which attempts to quantify some of these instabilities). As explained in Section 2 below, this finite co-dimension stability of the smooth implosion profiles is one of the fundamental obstacles in proving vorticity blowup. 1.3.3. Solutions evolving from a singular state While throughout this paper we are only concerned with smooth initial data, and we are only interested in the behavior of the resulting Euler solution up to the time of the first gradient singularity, there are many physically important problems in which the Euler evolution is initiated with data that already contains a singular state. A non-exhaustive list of examples includes the following:
the short time evolution of discontinuous shock fronts [ 60 , 59 ] ;
the shock reflection problem [ 14 , 18 ] ;
the shock implosion [ 44 ] and shock explosion [ 45 ] problems;
non-isentropic implosions with strictly positive pressure fields [ 46 , 47 ] ;
the stability of rarefaction waves evolving from multi-dimensional Riemann data [ 57 , 58 ] ;
the long-time stability of an irrotational expansion fan bounded by two shock surfaces [ 40 ] ;
and the ill-posedness of the Euler equations in multiple space dimensions for admissible weak solutions [ 26 , 51 , 32 ] . We also mention that there is a vast literature on transonic shocks for Euler both in the context of airfoils [ 64 , 15 ] and in the setting of nozzles [ 71 , 70 , 69 ] ; we refer the reader to the recent excellent review articles [ 65 , 19 , 17 ] for an extensive literature review. 1.3.4. Other singularities A complete classification of all possible singularities that multi-dimensional and smooth Euler dynamics can reach in finite time is, to date, elusive. As discussed above, it is known that for regular initial data, at the time $T$ of the first singularity the Euler solution is capable of developing a shock, an implosion, and the goal of this paper is to show that it can also develop a vorticity blowup. It remains unsettled if these are the only possible first singularities attainable by smooth Euler evolution (with the classical ideal gas law equation of state). It is, for example, an open problem to determine whether vacuum 10 10 10 By this we mean that at the time $T$ of the first singularity, the density $\rho(\cdot,T)$ is equal to $0$ , at at least one spatial point. can form in finite time from smooth, localized, and non-vacuous initial data, in multiple space dimensions. 11 11 11 In one space dimension, vacuum cannot dynamically arise form from nice initial data without a shock forming first [ 12 , 13 ] . 2. Main ideas of the proof and comparison with previous results Recall that in the two-dimensional setting of this paper, the specific vorticity $\omega/\rho$ is transported, see ( 1.2 ). In order to prove Theorem 1.1 , we construct initial data with non-trivial vorticity near $R=0$ , and for this data we construct an imploding solution by perturbing the radial implosion from [ 62 ] .
We emphasize that the finitely many unstable modes of the self-similar blowup profile from [ 62 ] can potentially lead to either trivial vorticity , or to the destruction of the smooth implosion mechanism .
To overcome this difficulty, we will crucially use the two-dimensional axisymmetric ansatz ( 1.4 ) and the structure of the equations ( 1.6 ), in order to establish full-stability of the swirl velocity , and hence of the vorticity. 2.1. Self-similar coordinates We introduce the self-similar coordinates, variables, and equations used in our analysis. For $r>1$ which satisfies ( 2.2 ) below, define the self-similar time and space coordinates via (2.1a) $s=\log\frac{1}{(T-t)^{1/r}},\quad y=\frac{x}{(T-t)^{1/r}},\quad\xi=|y|=\frac{R}{(T-t)^{1/r}}.$ Throughout the paper we will use $y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ to denote the self-similar vectorial space coordinate, and $\xi=|y|$ to denote the self-similar radial variable.
The self-similar velocity components and rescaled sound speed are then given by (2.1b) $\bigl(u^{R},u^{\theta},\sigma\bigr)(R,t)=\tfrac{1}{r}(T-t)^{\frac{1}{r}-1}\bigl(U,A,\Sigma\bigr)(\xi,s).$ It is convenient to also introduce the vectorial form of the self-similar velocity, that is (2.1c) $\mathbf{U}(y,s)=U(\xi,s)\mathbf{e}_{R},\quad\mathbf{A}(y,s)=A(\xi,s)\mathbf{e}_{\theta},$ where as before, $\xi=|y|$ .
Throughout the paper we will assume that the parameter $r$ appearing in ( 2.1a ) and ( 2.1b ) satisfies the following inequalities 12 12 12 The definition of $r_{\mathsf{eye}}$ is directly adapted from [ 62 ] , upon letting $d=2$ and $\gamma=1+2\alpha$ ; ( 2.2 ) is the same as [ 62 , equation (1.15)] . (2.2a) $\displaystyle 1<r<r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha),\qquad$ $\displaystyle r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha)=\tfrac{1+2\alpha}{1+\alpha\sqrt{2}},$ $\displaystyle\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2},$ (2.2b) $\displaystyle\tfrac{1+2\alpha}{1+\alpha\sqrt{2}}<r<r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha),\qquad$ $\displaystyle r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha)=1+\tfrac{\alpha}{(\sqrt{\alpha}+1)^{2}},$ $\displaystyle 0<\alpha<\tfrac{1}{2}.$ Using the vectorial self-similar variables introduced in ( 2.1c ), and with the self-similar rescaled sound speed from ( 2.1b ), the two-dimensional axisymmetric Euler system ( 1.6 ) may be rewritten as (2.3a) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathbf{U}+(r-1)\mathbf{U}+(y+\mathbf{U})\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}+\alpha\Sigma\nabla\Sigma=-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A},$ (2.3b) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathbf{A}+(r-1)\mathbf{A}+(y+\mathbf{U})\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A}=-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U},$ (2.3c) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\Sigma+(r-1)\Sigma+(y+\mathbf{U})\cdot\nabla\Sigma+\alpha\Sigma\,\operatorname{div}(\mathbf{U})=0.$ The initial data is prescribed at the self-similar time $s_{\mathsf{in}}\triangleq-\tfrac{1}{r}\log T,$ which corresponds to $t=0$ in ( 2.1a ). 2.2. Globally self-similar blowup profiles We summarize the results of [ 62 , Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.3] , which give the existence of smooth stationary self-similar profiles in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ , i.e. of smooth solutions $(\bar{\mathbf{U}},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ to ( 2.3 ) which are $s$ -independent. Theorem 2.1 ( Existence of smooth similarity profiles [ 62 ] ). There exists a (possibility empty) exceptional countable sequence $\Gamma=\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}$ whose accumulation points can only be $\{1,2,\infty\}$ such that the following holds. For each $\gamma\notin\Gamma$ ,
there exists a discrete sequence of blow up speeds $\{r_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}$ in the range ( 2.2 ), accumulating at $r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\frac{\gamma-1}{2})$ , such that the system ( 2.3 ) admits a $C^{\infty}$ -smooth radially symmetric stationary solution $\bar{\mathbf{U}}(y)=\bar{U}(\xi)\mathbf{e}_{R},\bar{\mathbf{A}}=0,\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)$ . The requirements on $\gamma$ and $r_{k}$ stated above are the same as in [ 62 , Theorem 1.2] , with $d=2$ . Throughout the paper we will fix a non-exceptional adiabatic exponent $\gamma\not\in\Gamma$ , a similarity exponent $r\in\{r_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}$ , and a similarity profile $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ in our analysis. We treat any constants related to the profile $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ , to $\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}$ , and to $r\in(1,r_{\mathsf{eye}}(\alpha))$ , as absolute constants. In the next lemma we collect some useful properties of the profiles $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ constructed in [ 62 ] . Lemma 2.2 ( Properties of the similarity profiles). The radial vector field $\bar{U}(\xi)\mathbf{e}_{R}$ and the radially symmetric function $\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)>0$ are smooth. For every integer $k\geq 0$ we have the following decay 13 13 13 Here and throughout the paper we denote by $\langle\cdot\rangle$ the quantity $\sqrt{1+|\cdot|^{2}}$ . as $\xi\to\infty$ : (2.4a) $|\partial^{k}_{\xi}\bar{U}(\xi)|\lesssim_{k}\langle\xi\rangle^{1-r-k},\quad|\partial^{k}_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)|\lesssim_{k}\langle\xi\rangle^{1-r-k}.$ There exists $\kappa>0$ and $\xi_{1}>\xi_{s}$ 14 14 14 The value $\xi_{s}$ corresponds to the point $P_{2}$ in the phase portrait of [ 62 ] or to the point $P_{s}$ in the 3D phase portrait of [ 5 ] . such that (2.4b) $\displaystyle 1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(\xi)-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)|$ $\displaystyle>\kappa,\quad\xi\in[0,\xi_{1}],$ (2.4c) $\displaystyle\xi+\bar{U}(\xi)-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)$ $\displaystyle>0,\quad\xi>\xi_{s},$ (2.4d) $\displaystyle 1+\xi^{-1}\bar{U}(\xi)$ $\displaystyle>\kappa,\quad\xi\geq 0.$ Lastly, since $r$ lies in the range ( 2.2 ), we have (2.4e) ${\lim}_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\xi^{-1}\bar{U}(\xi)=\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)=-\tfrac{r-1}{2\alpha}>-\tfrac{r}{2}.$ Properties ( 2.4 ) of the profiles $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ were either stated as is in [ 62 ] , or they follow from the proof given there. For instance, the outgoing property of the flow ( 2.4c ) follows from the phase portrait proved in [ 62 ] and continuity. The limit ( 2.4e ) has been proved in [ 62 ] . The interior repulsive property ( 2.4b ) is proved in [ 62 , Lemma 1.6] for $\xi\in[0,\xi_{s}]$ ; since the solution is smooth, there exists $\xi_{1}>\xi_{s}$ such the condition ( 2.4b ) holds for a domain $[0,\xi_{1}]$ strictly larger than $[0,\xi_{s}]$ . The decay property ( 2.4a ) was stated in [ 63 , Theorem 2.3] .
For the sake of completeness, we give a proof of Lemma 2.2 in Appendix B . Remark 2.3 ( No repulsive property in the far field). The repulsive property ( 2.4b ) may not hold true in the entire exterior domain $\xi>\xi_{s}$ for the 2D compressible Euler equations, which is related
to [ 62 , Lemma 1.7] . We also quote The Euler case in $d=2$ and $d=3$ for $l\leq\sqrt{3}$ requires special consideration.
In those cases, property (P) of (2.24), which ensures coercivity of the corresponding quadratic form in (6.14), does not hold for $Z>Z_{2}$ . from Section 8 on Page 846 in [ 63 ] , which suggests that the exterior repulsive property does not hold. The property (P) is related to the exterior repulsive property, and $Z_{2}$ corresponds to $\xi_{s}$ in ( 2.4b ). 2.3. Evolution of the perturbation We consider a solution to the two-dimensional axis-symmetric Euler system in self-similar coordinates ( 2.3 ) in perturbative form: (2.5) $(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{A},\Sigma)=(\bar{\mathbf{U}},0,\bar{\Sigma})+(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{A},\tilde{\Sigma})=(\bar{U}\,\mathbf{e}_{R},0,\bar{\Sigma})+(\tilde{U}\,\mathbf{e}_{R},A\,\mathbf{e}_{\theta},\tilde{\Sigma}).$ Our goal is to analyze the evolution of the perturbation (2.6a) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}$ $\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})+{{\mathcal{N}}}_{U}$ (2.6b) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathbf{A}$ $\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})+{\mathcal{N}}_{A}$ (2.6c) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tilde{\Sigma}$ $\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})+{\mathcal{N}}_{\Sigma}$ where the linearized operators are defined as (2.6d) $\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})={\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})=-(r-1)\tilde{\mathbf{U}}-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\tilde{\mathbf{U}}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}-\alpha\tilde{\Sigma}\nabla\bar{\Sigma}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\nabla\tilde{\Sigma}$ (2.6e) $\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})={\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})=-(r-1)\mathbf{A}-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}$ (2.6f) $\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\tilde{U},A,\tilde{\Sigma})={\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})=-(r-1)\tilde{\Sigma}-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\tilde{\Sigma}-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\nabla\bar{\Sigma}-\alpha\tilde{\Sigma}\operatorname{div}(\bar{\mathbf{U}})-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\operatorname{div}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}})$ and the nonlinear terms are defined as (2.7a) $\displaystyle{{\mathcal{N}}}_{U}=-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\mathbf{U}}-\alpha\tilde{\Sigma}\nabla\tilde{\Sigma}-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A}$ (2.7b) $\displaystyle{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}=-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\mathbf{U}}$ (2.7c) $\displaystyle{{\mathcal{N}}}_{\Sigma}=-\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\Sigma}-\alpha\tilde{\Sigma}\operatorname{div}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}).$ The initial data for the system ( 2.6 ) is specified at self-similar time $s_{\mathsf{in}}=\log T^{-1/r}$ . 2.4. Two key observations First, in the linearized equations around the profile $(\bar{U},0,\bar{\Sigma})$ , the evolution of
the perturbation $\mathbf{A}$ and those of the perturbation $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ are decoupled.
That is, in ( 2.6 ) the linearized operators ${\mathcal{L}}_{U}$ and ${\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}$ do not depend on $A$ .
Therefore, we can perform linear stability analysis of $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ and $\mathbf{A}$ separately. We will show that the linearized equations for $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ are stable upon modulating potentially finitely many unstable directions, developing a simpler stability proof. See Section 2.5 . Second, from ( 2.6b ) we deduce that the linearized equation satisfied by $A(\xi,s)/\xi$ is given by 15 15 15 Recall from ( 2.1c ) that $\mathbf{A}(y,s)=A(\xi,s)\mathbf{e}_{\theta}$ , and thus the self-similar vorticity at $\xi=0$ is related to $\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}A(\xi,s)/(2\xi)$ . We note that for a smooth perturbation, we have $A(0,s)=0$ and hence $A/\xi$ is well-defined. $\partial_{t}\Bigl(\frac{A}{\xi}\Bigr)+(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\Bigl(\frac{A}{\xi}\Bigr)=-\Bigl(r+2\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{A}{\xi}\Bigr).$ Our crucial observation is that the blowup profile satisfies $r+2(\bar{U}(\xi)/\xi)<0$ near $\xi=0$ , see ( 2.4e ), which suggests the linear stability of $A(\xi)/\xi$ near $\xi=0$ . Using this property, and the outgoing property of the self-similar velocity, namely that $\xi+\bar{U}(\xi)\geq\kappa\xi$ for for some $\kappa>0$ (see ( 2.4d )), we obtain that $A$ is stable in a suitably weighted space. These stability properties can also be captured by performing weighted $L^{2}$ estimates with weights that are singular near $\xi=0$ . See Theorem 3.2 . In order to prove Theorem 1.1 , we note that since the radial velocity $\bar{\mathbf{U}}+\tilde{\mathbf{U}}$ has $0$ vorticity, and since $\mathbf{A}$ is stable, we can choose initial data for $\mathbf{A}$ in an open set (in the topology of a weighted Sobolev space) and thus construct non-trivial initial vorticity. Moreover, since $(\mathbf{U}+\tilde{\mathbf{U}}+\mathbf{A})(0,s)=0$ , the origin is a stationary point of the flow; thus, once we prove global nonlinear stability for ( 2.6 )–( 2.7 ), the finite-time blowup of vorticity claimed in ( 1.8d ) now follows. 2.5. A new stability proof of implosion The goal of this paper is to also develop a new stability proof of the implosion for the compressible Euler equations, which is simpler than the one given in [ 63 , 61 ] (based on estimates of nonlinear wave equation), and in [ 5 ] (based on the transport structure of Riemann-type variables). While in this paper we focus on the two-dimensional setting, the stability proof for the Euler equations in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ with radially symmetric data (around a profile satisfying the properties in Lemma 2.2 ) would be the same, by setting $\mathbf{A}=0$ in Sections 3 , and 4 . Our stability proof consists of the following steps. Step 1: Weighted energy estimates In the linear stability analysis of Section 3.2 we perform weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates, with sufficiently large $m$ and a carefully designed weight $\varphi_{2m}$ , to obtain coercive estimates for the “top order” terms. The interior repulsive property ( 2.4b ) allows us to obtain a damping term proportional to $m$ in the region $|y|=\xi\leq\xi_{1}$ , slightly beyond the sonic point $\xi_{s}<\xi_{1}$ .
For $|y|=\xi>\xi_{s}$ , we use the outgoing property related to the transport term ( 2.4c ). We design a radially symmetric weight $\varphi_{2m}(y)$ decreasing in $|y|=\xi$ for $\xi\in[\xi_{1},R_{2}]$ , with $R_{2}$ large enough.
The monotonicity of the weight and the outgoing property provides a strong damping term in the energy estimates, as seen in Lemma 3.1 .
For $|y|=\xi>R_{2}$ , due to the natural decay of the profile ( 2.4a ), the linear dynamics is purely governed by the scaling terms, and is thus stable. The upshot of the linear stability analysis is that we obtain weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates of the kind (2.8) $\langle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma),(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq-\lambda\|(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\int_{|y|\leq R_{4}}{\mathcal{R}}_{m}dy,$ for some inner product ${\mathcal{X}}^{m},m\geq m_{0}$ with $m_{0}$ sufficiently large, a suitably chosen $R_{4}>0$ and a coercive parameter $\lambda>0$ (uniform in $m\geq m_{0}$ ).
Here, ${\mathcal{R}}_{m}$ denotes some “lower order” remainder terms (in terms of regularity, when compared to $\|(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ ), which is made explicit in ( 3.13a ).
For the stability estimates of the $\mathbf{A}$ variable, we use the ideas discussed earlier in Section 2.4 . Step 2: Compact perturbation To handle the lower order remainder term ${\mathcal{R}}_{m}$ in ( 2.8 ), for each $m\geq m_{0}$ , in Section 3.3 we use
Riesz representation to construct a linear operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ from a bilinear form similar to $\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=\int\chi f\cdot gdy,$ where $\chi\geq 0$ is a smooth cutoff function with $\chi=1$ for $|y|\leq R_{4}$ . The second term on the right side of ( 2.8 ) then arises as $\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ for $f=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ . Since the right hand side in the above display does not involve derivatives on $f$ and $g$ , and since $\chi$ has a compact support, we can show that ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ is compact and that it has a smoothing effect ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ . Afterwards, we obtain that ${\mathcal{L}}-\bar{C}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is dissipative , for $\bar{C}>0$ chosen large enough. Step 3: Construction of semigroup To handle the compact perturbation, in Sections 3.4 and 3.5 we use semigroup theory to estimate the growth bound for the semigroups $e^{({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})s}$ and $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}$ . We prove a local well-posedness (LWP) result of the linearized equations in the space ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ in order to construct the semigroup. Note that the linearized equations are a symmetric hyperbolic system, whose LWP theory is classical. Step 4: Smoothness of unstable direction To construct a smooth initial data, in Section 3.6 we show that the potentially unstable directions of the linearized operators are spanned by smooth functions. An unstable direction $f$ satisfies a variant of the equation $({\mathcal{L}}-\lambda)f=0$ , for $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m_{0}}$ . Rewriting this as $({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}-\lambda)f=-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f$ , the smoothing effect of ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ , the invertibility $({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}-\lambda)^{-1}\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ ,
and induction on $m\geq m_{0}$ , allows us to prove prove $f\in C^{\infty}$ . See Lemma 3.9 . We note that Steps 2 & 4 discussed above resemble the functional approach to solve the elliptic equations, see e.g. [ 38 ] . The coercive estimates ( 2.8 ) allow us to construct the compact perturbation, which has a smoothing effect. These steps do not depend on specific structure of the equations. Step 5: Nonlinear estimates To close the nonlinear stability estimates, in Section 4 we decompose the perturbation $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{A},\tilde{\Sigma})$ into a stable part $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ and an unstable part $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ , following the splitting method of [ 22 ] . For the unstable part, we represent $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ using Duhamel’s formula and estimate it using the semigroup $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}$ .
The stable part $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ is estimated purely using energy estimates. Such a decomposition allows us to construct the global in self-similar time solution using a Banach fixed point argument and simpler bootstrap assumptions; moreover, this decomposition implicitly determines the stable initial data in the correct finite co-dimension space. See [ 48 , 36 ] for related constructions of a global-in-time self-similar solution based on a Banach fixed point argument. 2.6. Comparison with the stability estimates in [ 63 ] , [ 61 ] and [ 5 ] Part of our stability estimates are similar to those in [ 63 ] , [ 61 ] , and in [ 5 ] : high order weighted Sobolev estimates to extract the dissipation,
semigroup theory to estimate the compact perturbation, and finite co-dimension stability. Moreover, our $H^{2m}$ stability estimates based on the variable $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ are inspired by [ 5 ] particularly in the region $|y|=\xi\leq\xi_{s}$ . There are however several significant differences between our estimates and those in [ 63 , 61 , 5 ] . First, we do not localize the linearized operator, nor do we use Riemann-type variables to perform transport estimates. Instead, we
exploit the outgoing properties of the flow ( 2.4c ) by designing decreasing weights and incorporate such an effect in the weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates. This allows us to obtain simpler nonlinear estimates. We note that weighted energy estimates with singular weights and weights depending on the profiles have been used successfully in a series of works by the first author and collaborators [ 24 , 21 , 25 , 22 , 20 ] . Second, we construct the semigroup based on the local well-posedness (LWP) of the linearized equations, which is obtained by appealing to a classical result.
See Step 2 mentioned above, and Theorem 3.5 .
In contrast, in [ 5 , 61 ] , the construction is based on the notion of a maximal dissipative operator. Moreover, using the functional approach outlined in Step 4 above, we do not need to solve specific ODE or PDE problems as [ 5 , 61 ] , and hence we obtain simpler linear stability estimates. Third, we use a Banach fixed point argument to obtain nonlinear finite co-dimension stability, different from the topological arguments employed in [ 5 , 62 ] . Finally, we do not use the exterior repulsive property of the profile in the stability proof, which is required in [ 5 ] , but may not hold for the similarity profile of 2D Euler equations (see the discussion in Remark 2.3 ). Thus, we cannot adopt the stability proof in [ 5 ] to establish the finite co-dimension stability of $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ . Our stability estimates can be extended to the compressible Navier-Stokes equations in the same settings as in [ 5 , 63 ] ,
with a minor modification since the diffusion is treated perturbatively, and the main term of the diffusion in the weighted estimates still has a good sign after performing integration by parts. 2.7. Comparison with the non-radial implosion in [ 11 ] There appear to be essential difficulties in using the non-radial imploding solutions built in [ 11 ] to exhibit vorticity blowup for the compressible Euler equations in three space dimensions. We recall that for $d=3$ , the specific vorticity of isentropic Euler satisfies the vector-transport equation (2.9) $\partial_{t}\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)=(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr).$ If we denote by $X(a,t)$ the Lagrangian flow 16 16 16 The Lagrangian flow $X$ solves the ODE $\partial_{t}X(a,t)=u(X(t,a),t)$ , with initial condition $X(a,0)=a$ . generated by $u$ , then ( 2.9 ) can be rewritten as (2.10) $\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)(X(a,t),t)=\nabla_{a}X(a,t)\Bigl(\frac{\omega}{\rho}\Bigr)(a,0).$ If we imagine $\mathbf{u}$ and $\sigma$ (and hence $\rho=(\alpha\sigma)^{1/\alpha}$ ) to be exactly the self-similar solutions to ( 1.3 ) built in [ 62 ] and [ 5 ] , then the vorticity $\omega$ would just be identically $0$ by radial symmetry. Nevertheless, we can replace the $\omega(X(a,t),t)$ appearing on the right side of ( 2.10 ) with a generic quantity $f=f(X(a,t),t)=\rho(X(a,t),t)\nabla_{a}X(a,t)\tfrac{f_{0}}{\rho_{0}}(a),$ and ask whether $\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}}$ blows up as $t\scalebox{0.6}{$\nearrow$}T$ , with $T$ being the time of the implosion. In the next lemma we observe that $f$ remains uniformly bounded in $a$ and $t$ , as long as we choose the initial data $f_{0}$ with a high-enough vanishing order at $a=0$ . Lemma 2.4 ( Not all transported quantities blow up in a 3D implosion). Consider a self-similar profile $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ as constructed in [ 62 ] or [ 5 ] , and let $(\mathbf{u},\sigma)$ be determined according to exact self-similarity from this profile via ( 2.1 ).
Then, there exists $k_{\sf van}>0$ such that for every smooth function $f_{0}$ that satisfies $|f_{0}(a)|\lesssim\min(|a|^{k_{\sf van}},1)$ , we have $\sup_{a\in\mathbb{R}^{3},t\in[0,T)}|\rho(X(a,t),t)\nabla_{a}X(a,t)\tfrac{f_{0}}{\rho_{0}}(a)|<+\infty.$ The required vanishing order $k_{\sf van}$ is some constant that depends only on the profile $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ , on $r$ , and on $\alpha$ . We defer the proof to Appendix A .
At this stage we note that the finite co-dimension of the initial data considered in [ 11 ] equals to the number of unstable directions of ${\mathcal{L}}$ , which is a (sufficiently large) finite rank perturbation ${\mathcal{K}}$ to a maximally dissipative operator. Since the rank of ${\mathcal{K}}$ is used as a large parameter, the number of unstable directions $k_{\sf uns}$ is not quantified in [ 11 ] , and therefore it is hard to determine whether $k_{\sf uns}<k_{\sf van}$ . Moreover, it is not shown in [ 11 ] that the finite co-dimension set of initial data contain any functions $f_{0}$ violating the vanishing order $f_{0}=\mathcal{O}(|a|^{k_{\sf van}})$ near $a=0$ .
Therefore, in light of Lemma 2.4 it is not clear whether the setting of [ 11 ] is at all amenable to vorticity blowup. 2.8. Notation Next, we discuss the notation used in the paper. We use $A\lesssim B$ to mean that there exists a constant $C=C(\gamma,r,\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma},T)\geq 1$ such that $A\leq CB$ . We use $A\asymp B$ to mean that both $A\lesssim B$ and $B\lesssim A$ .
We use $A\triangleq B$ to say that quantity $A$ is defined to be equal to quantity/expression $B$ . In Section 4 we shall use the notation $A=B+\mathcal{O}_{h}(B^{\prime})$ if there exists $C_{h}=C(\gamma,r,\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma},T,h)\geq 1$ such that $|A-B|\leq C_{\ast}B^{\prime}$ . We will use $\eta,\eta_{s},\lambda,\lambda_{1}$ to denote constants related to the decay rates; see ( 3.47 ), ( 3.48 ), and ( 4.9 ).
We use $\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}$ to denote exponents for the weights; see ( 3.5 ), ( 3.15 ), and ( 3.20 ). We use $\varphi_{1},\varphi_{m},\varphi_{b},\varphi_{f},\varphi_{g},\varphi_{A}$ to denote various weights; see ( 3.5 ), ( 3.6 ), ( 3.15 ), ( 3.28 ), and use $E_{k},E_{\infty}$ for energy estimates ( 4.22 ), ( 4.50 ). We use ${\mathcal{X}}^{m},{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{A},{\mathcal{W}}^{m},{\mathcal{W}}^{m}_{A},Y_{1},Y_{2}$ to denote various functional spaces; see ( 3.14 ), ( 4.7 ), and ( 4.13 ). We will use $c,C$ to denote absolute constants that can vary from line to line, and use $\mu,\nu,\varepsilon_{m},\bar{C},\mu_{m},\delta,\delta_{Y}$ to denote some absolute constants that do not change from line to line. Throughout Section 3 , we shall use $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ to denote the perturbation $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ mentioned earlier in ( 2.5 ), since for linear stability analysis there is no ambiguity. In contrast, in the nonlinear stability of Section 4 , $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ is reserved for the full radial velocity and rescaled sound speed, consistently with ( 2.5 ) and ( 2.3 ). 3. Linear stability In this section, we perform weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates and study the
asymptotic behavior of the linear operators ${\mathcal{L}}_{U}$ , ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ , and ${\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}$ defined in ( 2.6 ). The design of the weights is done in Section 3.1 , while the Sobolev energy estimates are performed in Section 3.2 . 3.1. Choice of weights In this section, we design the weight $\varphi_{2m}$ needed for weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates. Lemma 3.1 . There exists a radially symmetric weight $\varphi_{1}(y)$ , and there exists a constant $\mu_{1}>0$ , such that (3.1a) $\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\asymp\langle\xi\rangle,\quad|\nabla\varphi_{1}|\lesssim 1,\quad\xi=|y|,$ (3.1b) $\displaystyle\frac{(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+\ell\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\Big|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\Big|-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\ell\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\leq-\frac{\mu_{1}}{\langle\xi\rangle},$ for all $\xi\in(0,\infty)$ and all $\ell\in\{0,1\}$ .
For $m\geq 1$ and $\varphi_{1}$ satisfying ( 3.1 ), define (3.2) $\varphi_{m}(y)=\varphi_{1}(y)^{m}.$ The quantity on the left hand side of ( 3.1b ) appears in the damping term in the weighted $H^{2k}$ estimates. The first term comes from the effect of the transport term. The last two terms come from the derivatives. See ( 3.21 ), ( 3.23 ), ( 3.24 ). Proof. Since $\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\geq 0$ , we only need to prove the estimate for $\ell=1$ . To design the weights, we will introduce several parameters and determine them in the following order (3.3) $R_{1}\rightsquigarrow R_{2}\rightsquigarrow c_{1}\rightsquigarrow R_{3}\rightsquigarrow\kappa_{2}\rightsquigarrow\nu\rightsquigarrow\mu_{1},$ with parameters appearing later being allowed to depend on the previous ones. Since we fix the profiles $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ and related parameters (e.g., $\kappa,\xi_{1},\xi_{s}$ in Lemma 2.2 ), we drop the dependence of $R_{1}$ and $R_{2}$ from ( 3.3 ) on these parameters. Recall the parameters $\xi_{s},\xi_{1}$ from Lemma 2.2 . We fix $R_{1}$ with $1<\xi_{s}<R_{1}<\xi_{1}$ . From Lemma 2.2 , since $\partial_{\xi}\bar{U},\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}$ decay, we can choose $R_{2}$ such that for $\xi>R_{2}$ , we have (3.4) $\partial_{\xi}(\xi+\bar{U})-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|>\tfrac{1}{2},\quad\xi>R_{2}.$ For $\kappa_{2},\nu>0$ to be determined (see ( 3.9 ) below), we construct the weights as (3.5) $\varphi_{m}(y)\triangleq\varphi_{1}(y)^{m},\qquad\varphi_{1}\triangleq\varphi_{b}^{\kappa_{2}}\varphi_{f},\qquad\varphi_{f}(y)\triangleq 1+\nu\langle y\rangle,$ where $\varphi_{b}$ is chosen in ( 3.6 ) below. Here the sub-indices b, f are short for bulk , and far , respectively.
We use the bulk part to capture the outgoing effect ( 2.4c ) for $|y|>\xi_{s}$ , and the far part to capture the decay of the perturbation. With $\xi_{s}<R_{1}<\xi_{1}$ and $R_{2}$ that have already been chosen, there exists $c_{1}=c_{1}(R_{1},R_{2})>0$ which allows us to define $\varphi_{b}$ as a radially symmetric function with (3.6a) $\displaystyle\varphi_{b}(y)$ $\displaystyle=1,\qquad|y|\leq\xi_{s},$ (3.6b) $\displaystyle\varphi_{b}(y)$ $\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2},\qquad|y|\geq R_{2}+1,$ (3.6c) $\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}$ $\displaystyle\leq 0,\qquad\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},$ (3.6d) $\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}\leq-c_{1}$ $\displaystyle<0,\qquad|y|\in[R_{1},R_{2}].$ Denote the self-similar slow acoustic wave speed by $\bar{V}=\xi+\bar{U}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}.$ From ( 2.4a ), we can choose $R_{3}$ large enough such that (3.7) $-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\leq-1+\tfrac{1}{8}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1},\quad\bar{V}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\leq\xi+\tfrac{1}{8},\quad\forall\xi\geq R_{3}.$ Next, we let $D_{wg}\triangleq\frac{(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\Big|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\Big|=\frac{\bar{V}\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\left(\Big|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\Big|+\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\right).$ Using the definition of $\varphi_{1}$ in ( 3.5 ), the monotonicity properties $\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}\leq 0$ and $\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{f}\geq 0$ , and the identity $\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}=\kappa_{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}}{\varphi_{b}}+\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{f}}{\varphi_{f}}$ , we deduce (3.8a) $\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\Big|+\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}\leq 2\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{f}}{\varphi_{f}}=\frac{2\nu\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle(1+\nu\langle\xi\rangle)},$ (3.8b) $\displaystyle D_{wg}\leq\kappa_{2}\frac{\bar{V}\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}}{\varphi_{b}}+\frac{\bar{V}\nu\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle(1+\nu\langle\xi\rangle)}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\frac{\nu\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle(1+\nu\langle\xi\rangle)}.$ Next, our goal is to find positive constants $\kappa_{2}$ (see ( 3.10 )) and $\nu$ (see ( 3.11 )), in order to ensure that (3.9a) $\displaystyle D_{0}$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\kappa_{2}\frac{\bar{V}\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}}{\varphi_{b}}-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\leq-c<0,$ (3.9b) $\displaystyle D_{1}$ $\displaystyle\triangleq D_{wg}-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\leq D_{0}+\frac{(\bar{V}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma})\nu\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle(1+\nu\langle\xi\rangle)}\leq-\frac{\mu_{1}}{\langle\xi\rangle},$ hold for some constants $\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\kappa_{2},\nu)>0$ and $c=c(R_{1},R_{2})>0$ .
Here we recall that $\bar{V}=\xi+\bar{U}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}>0$ due to ( 2.4c ).
Note that the estimate in ( 3.9b ) is exactly the same as ( 3.1b ). In order to achieve ( 3.9 ), we first note that $\bar{V}\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}\leq 0$ by the definition of $\varphi_{b}$ in ( 3.6 ).
For $\xi\in[0,\xi_{1}]$ and $\xi\geq R_{2}$ , using the repulsive property ( 2.4b ) and inequality ( 3.4 ), we get $D_{0}\leq-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\leq\max(-\kappa,-\tfrac{1}{2})<0.$ For $\xi\in[\xi_{1},R_{2}]$ , from ( 2.4c ) we get $\bar{V}>c_{2}>0$ for some $c_{2}=c_{2}(R_{2})>0$ , and from ( 3.6 ) we have $\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}/\varphi_{b}\leq-c_{1}(R_{1},R_{2})<0$ ; together, these bounds imply $\frac{\bar{V}\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{b}}{\varphi_{b}}<-c_{1}c_{2}<0.$ Since $|1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}||\leq C$ holds by ( 2.4a ), choosing $\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{2}(R_{1},R_{2})$ large enough, (3.10) $\quad D_{0}<-\kappa_{2}c_{1}c_{2}+C=-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}c_{1}c_{2}\triangleq-c$ for some $c=(R_{1},R_{2})>0$ , as claimed in ( 3.9a ). In order to prove ( 3.9b ), we note that for $\nu\leq 1$ , using ( 3.7 ) we get that for all $\xi\geq R_{3}$ we have $\displaystyle D_{1}$ $\displaystyle\leq-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)+\frac{(\bar{V}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma})\nu\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle(1+\nu\langle\xi\rangle)}\leq-1+\frac{1}{8\langle\xi\rangle}+\frac{\nu\xi^{2}}{\langle\xi\rangle+\nu(1+\xi^{2})}+\frac{1}{8\langle\xi\rangle}$ $\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\langle\xi\rangle+\nu}{\langle\xi\rangle+\nu(1+\xi^{2})}+\frac{1}{4\langle\xi\rangle}\leq-\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{2\langle\xi\rangle^{2}}+\frac{1}{4\langle\xi\rangle}=-\frac{1}{4\langle\xi\rangle}.$ For $\xi\in[0,R_{3}]$ , using ( 3.9a ) and choosing $\nu=\nu(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})>0$ small enough, we get (3.11) $D_{1}\leq D_{0}+\nu\|\bar{V}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,R_{3}]}\leq-c+\nu\|\bar{V}+2\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,R_{3}]}<-\tfrac{1}{2}c.$ Combining the above two estimates, we establish ( 3.9b ) for some $\mu_{1}(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\kappa_{2},\nu)>0$ . This concludes the proof of ( 3.1b ). It remains to establish ( 3.1a ).
Since $\varphi_{b}$ is constant for $|y|\leq\xi_{s}$ and $|y|\geq R_{2}+1$ ,
from the definition ( 3.5 ), we get $\varphi_{1}\asymp\varphi_{f}$ , $|\nabla\varphi_{1}|\asymp|\nabla\varphi_{f}|\lesssim 1$ in this region; the implicit constants depend on $R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\nu,\kappa_{2}$ which have been already fixed. On the compact set $\xi\in[\xi_{s},R_{2}+1]$ , the claim ( 3.1a ) follows since $\varphi_{f}$ and $\varphi_{b}$ (hence $\varphi_{1}$ ) are $C^{1}$ smooth. MM $\square$ 3.2. Weighted $H^{2m}$ Coercive estimates Throughout this section, since we are only concerned with linear stability analysis, we will use $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ to denote the perturbation $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma})$ ; that is, we drop the $\tilde{\cdot}$ in ( 2.6 ). Recall the weights $\varphi_{m}$ defined in ( 3.5 ) and recall the definitions ( 2.6d )–( 2.6f ) of the linearized operators ${\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ , and ${\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}$ . Note that the operators (3.12) ${\mathcal{L}}\triangleq({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})$ and ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ are fully decoupled, in the sense that $A$ does not enter in the definition of ${\mathcal{L}}$ , and $(U,\Sigma)$ do not enter in the definition of ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ ; see ( 2.6 ). With this notation, we have the following coercive estimates. Theorem 3.2 . Denote ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})$ , with $({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})$ as defined in ( 2.6 ). There exists $m_{0}\geq 6$ , $R_{4},\bar{C}>0$ large enough and $\lambda>0$ small enough, 17 17 17 The parameters $m_{0},R_{4},\bar{C}$ and $\lambda$ depend only on the weight $\varphi_{1}$ from Lemma 3.1 , on $\gamma>1$ , $r>0$ , and on the profiles $(\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma})$ . such that the following statements hold true. For any $m\geq m_{0}$ , there exists $\varepsilon_{m}=\varepsilon_{m}(m_{0},R_{4},\bar{C},\lambda)>0$ such that (3.13a) $\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma),(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ $\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\|(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\bar{C}\int_{|y|\leq R_{4}}(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g}dy,$ (3.13b) $\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A}),\mathbf{A}\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}}$ $\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\|\mathbf{A}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}}^{2},$ for any $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in D({\mathcal{L}})=\{(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\colon{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\}$ and $\mathbf{A}\in D({\mathcal{L}}_{A})=\{\mathbf{A}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{A}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{A}\}$ . Here, the Hilbert spaces ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}$ are defined as the completion of the space of $C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ radially-symmetric 18 18 18 By radially symmetric functions we mean $f(y)=f(|y|)$ and by radially symmetric vectors we mean $\boldsymbol{f}(y)=f(|y|)\mathbf{e}_{R}$ or $\boldsymbol{f}(y)=f(|y|)\mathbf{e}_{\theta}$ ; recall the definition ( 1.4 ). scalar/vector functions, with respect to the norm induced by the inner products (3.14a) $\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}:=\varepsilon_{m}\int\Delta^{m}f\cdot\Delta^{m}g\;\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+f\cdot g\;\varphi_{g}dy,\ m\geq 1,\quad\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}:=\int f\cdot g\;\varphi_{g},$ (3.14b) $\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}}:=\varepsilon_{m}\int\Delta^{m}f\cdot\Delta^{m}g\;\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+f\cdot g\;\varphi_{A}dy,\ m\geq 1,\quad\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{0}}:=\int f\cdot g\;\varphi_{A},$ where $f,g:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\ell\in\{1,2\}$ . 19 19 19 It is also convenient to denote ${\mathcal{X}}^{\infty}=\cap_{m\geq 0}{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . The inner products $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ and $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}}$ , and the associated norms, are defined in terms of the constants $\varepsilon_{m}$ (defined in the last paragraph of Section 3.2.1 ), the weight $\varphi_{2m}=\varphi_{1}^{2m}$ defined in ( 3.2 ), in terms of the weight (3.15) $\varphi_{g}=\varphi_{g}(y)\triangleq\langle y\rangle^{-\kappa_{1}-2},\quad\kappa_{1}=\tfrac{1}{4},$ and in terms of the weight $\varphi_{A}$ constructed in ( 3.28 ) below. At this stage we note that $\varphi_{A}$ satisfies $\varphi_{A}(y)\asymp|y|^{-\beta_{1}}\langle y\rangle^{\beta_{1}-\kappa_{1}-2}$ for some $\beta_{1}\in(3,4)$ , and thus $\varphi_{g}\asymp\varphi_{A}$ for $|y|\geq 1$ , and $\varphi_{g}\lesssim\varphi_{A}$ for all $|y|>0$ . We emphasize that the constants $\lambda,\bar{C}$ appearing in ( 3.13a ) are independent of $m$ .
Note also that the norms ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}$ only differ by the weighted $L^{2}$ parts, i.e. $\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}-\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}}^{2}=\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}-\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}_{A}}^{2}$ , see ( 3.14 ). Before proving Theorem 3.2 , we note the following simple nestedness property of the spaces ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , which follows from Lemmas C.2 , C.3 with $\delta_{1}=1,\delta_{2}=-2-\kappa_{1}$ . Lemma 3.3 . For $n>m$ , we have $\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim_{n}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{n}}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}^{n}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Next, we turn to the proof of Theorem 3.2 . Since ${\mathcal{L}}$ and ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ are decoupled (cf. ( 2.6 ) and ( 3.12 )), we prove ( 3.13a )
and ( 3.13b ) separately, in Sections 3.2.1 and 3.2.2 , respectively. 3.2.1. Estimates for ${\mathcal{L}}$ Applying the operator $\Delta^{m}$ to the linearized operators ${\mathcal{L}}_{U}={\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ and ${\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ defined in ( 2.6d ), ( 2.6f ), and using Lemma C.1 to extract the leading order parts from the terms containing $\nabla\mathbf{U},\nabla\Sigma$ ,
we get $\displaystyle\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{U}$ $\displaystyle=\underbrace{-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\nabla\Delta^{m}\Sigma}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{U}}\underbrace{-(r-1)\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}-2m\partial_{\xi}(\xi+\bar{U})\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}-2m\alpha\nabla\bar{\Sigma}\Delta^{m}\Sigma}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{U}}$ (3.16a) $\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{-\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}-\alpha\Delta^{m}\Sigma\nabla\bar{\Sigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{U}}+{\mathcal{R}}_{U,m},$ $\displaystyle\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}$ $\displaystyle=\underbrace{-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{m}\Sigma-\alpha\operatorname{div}(\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U})\bar{\Sigma}}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}}\underbrace{-(r-1)\Delta^{m}\Sigma-2m\partial_{\xi}(\xi+\bar{U})\Delta^{m}\Sigma-2m\alpha\nabla\bar{\Sigma}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\Sigma}}$ (3.16b) $\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{-\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\Sigma}-\alpha\operatorname{div}(\bar{\mathbf{U}})\Delta^{m}\Sigma}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}}+{\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,m}.$ In ( 3.16 ) we have denoted by ${\mathcal{R}}_{U,m}$ and ${\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,m}$ remainder terms which are of lower order (in terms of highest derivative count on an individual term); moreover, we have used the notation ${\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{S}}$ to single out transport , dissipative , and stretching terms. Using Lemma C.1 and the decay estimates ( 2.4a ), we obtain that the remainder terms are bounded as $\displaystyle|{\mathcal{R}}_{U,m}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}|\nabla^{2m+1-i}\bar{\mathbf{U}}|\;|\nabla^{i}\mathbf{U}|+|\nabla^{2m+1-i}\bar{\Sigma}|\;|\nabla^{i}\Sigma|$ (3.17a) $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}\langle y\rangle^{-r-2m+i}(|\nabla^{i}\mathbf{U}|+|\nabla^{i}\Sigma|),$ $\displaystyle|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,m}|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}|\nabla^{2m+1-i}\bar{\mathbf{U}}|\;|\nabla^{i}\Sigma|+|\nabla^{2m+1-i}\bar{\Sigma}|\;|\nabla^{i}\mathbf{U}|$ (3.17b) $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}\langle y\rangle^{-r-2m+i}(|\nabla^{i}\mathbf{U}|+|\nabla^{i}\Sigma|).$ Next, in order to bound the left side of ( 3.13a ), we perform weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates with weight given by $\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ , as dictated by the definitions in ( 3.13a ). To this end, we estimate the term (3.18) $\int(\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\,\Delta^{m}\Sigma)\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g},$ by appealing to the decomposition in ( 3.16 ). Estimate for ${\mathcal{T}}_{U},{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}$ Using the identity (3.19) $\nabla\Delta^{m}\Sigma\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+\operatorname{div}(\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U})\Delta^{m}\Sigma=\nabla\cdot(\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}\cdot\Delta^{m}\Sigma)$ and integration by parts, we obtain that the contribution of the transport terms in ( 3.16 ) to the expression ( 3.18 ) is given by $\displaystyle I_{{\mathcal{T}}}$ $\displaystyle=-\int\Big((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{m}\Sigma\cdot\Delta^{m}\Sigma$ $\displaystyle+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{m}\Sigma\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\operatorname{div}(\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U})\Delta^{m}\Sigma\Big)\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ $\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla\cdot((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g})}{\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}}(|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Delta^{m}\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+\frac{\nabla(\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g})}{\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}\Delta^{m}\Sigma\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}.$ Recall from ( 3.2 ) and ( 3.15 ) that $\varphi_{2m}=\varphi_{1}^{2m}$ , and $\varphi_{g}=\langle y\rangle^{-2-\kappa_{1}}$ . Using the decay estimates in ( 2.4a ), the outgoing property $\xi+\bar{U}>0$ ( 2.4d ),
and denoting 20 20 20 The fact that $r<2$ follows since for $d=2$ (the case of this paper) the inequality $r<r_{\sf eye}(\alpha)$ implies $r<2$ , see ( 2.2 ). (3.20) $\kappa_{3}=\min(2,r)=r>1,$ we obtain $\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{g}}{\varphi_{g}}=(-2-\kappa_{1})\frac{\xi}{1+\xi^{2}},\quad(\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{g}}{\varphi_{g}}\leq-2-\kappa_{1}+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-2}+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-r}\leq-2+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}.$ Using the above inequality and ( 2.4a ) (with $k=1$ ), we estimate $\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla\cdot((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g})}{\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}}$ $\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(2+\operatorname{div}(\bar{\mathbf{U}})+4m(\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+(\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{g}}{\varphi_{g}}\Big)$ $\displaystyle\leq C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}+2m(\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}},$ $\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\nabla(\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g})}{\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}}\Big|$ $\displaystyle\leq\alpha|\nabla\bar{\Sigma}|+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\Bigl(4m\frac{|\nabla\varphi_{1}|}{\varphi_{1}}+\frac{|\nabla\varphi_{g}|}{\varphi_{g}}\Bigr)\leq 4m\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\frac{|\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}|}{\varphi_{1}}+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}},$ where we have used $|\nabla f|=|\partial_{\xi}f|$ for any radially symmetric function $f$ . Using the bound $\langle y\rangle^{2(2m+\delta_{2})}\lesssim_{m}\langle y\rangle^{-r}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ , and using the fact that $\varepsilon>0$ is arbitrary, we get $\displaystyle I_{{\mathcal{R}}}$ $\displaystyle=\int\bigl|{\mathcal{R}}_{U,m}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+{\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,m}\cdot\Delta^{m}\Sigma\bigr|\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ (3.24) $\displaystyle\leq\int\langle\xi\rangle^{-r}\Big((|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Delta^{m}\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+C_{m}(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})\varphi_{g}\Big).$ Combining the bounds ( 3.21 ), ( 3.23 ), ( 3.24 ), using the estimates in Lemma 3.1 , and recalling ( 3.20 ),
we arrive at $\displaystyle\int(\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\,\Delta^{m}\Sigma)\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}=I_{{\mathcal{T}}}+I_{{\mathcal{D}}+{\mathcal{S}}}+I_{{\mathcal{R}}}$ $\displaystyle\leq\int\left(-(r-1)+2m\Big((\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}|-(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}-\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|)\Big)+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\right)$ $\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times(|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Delta^{m}\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+C_{m}\int(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g}$ $\displaystyle\leq\int\left(-(r-1)-2m\mu_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}+a_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\right)(|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Delta^{m}\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+C_{m}(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g},$ for some constant $a_{1}>0$ .
Since $\kappa_{3}=r>1$ (see ( 3.20 )), there exists $m_{0}$ sufficiently large, e.g. $m_{0}=\lceil\frac{a_{1}}{2\mu_{1}}\rceil+1$ , such that for any $m\geq m_{0}$ , we get $-(r-1)-2m\mu_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}+a_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\leq-(r-1)-2m_{0}\mu_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}+a_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\leq-(r-1),$ resulting in $\displaystyle\int(\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}+\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\,\Delta^{m}\Sigma)\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ (3.25) $\displaystyle\quad\leq-(r-1)\int(|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Delta^{m}\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+C_{m}(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g}.$ Weighted $L^{2}$ estimates For $m=0$ , we do not have the lower order terms ${\mathcal{R}}_{U,0},{\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,0}$ in ( 3.17 ) and we do not need to estimate $I_{{\mathcal{R}}}$ as in ( 3.24 ). Combining ( 3.21 ) and ( 3.23 ) (with $m=0$ ), we obtain $\int({\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\mathbf{U}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma)\varphi_{g}\leq\int(-(r-1)+\bar{C}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}})(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g},$ for some constant $\bar{C}>0$ , independent of $m$ .
Since $r>1$ and $\kappa_{3}>0$ , there exists a sufficiently large $R_{4}$ such that for all $\xi=|y|\geq R_{4}$ we have $-(r-1)+\bar{C}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\leq-(r-1)+\bar{C}\langle R_{4}\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}(r-1).$ Therefore, combining the two estimates above, we arrive at (3.26) $\int({\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\mathbf{U}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma)\varphi_{g}\leq\int\bigl(\bar{C}\mathbf{1}_{|y|\leq R_{4}}-\tfrac{1}{2}(r-1)\bigr)(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+|\Sigma|^{2})\varphi_{g}.$ Choosing the $\varepsilon_{m}$ In order to conclude the proof of ( 3.13a ), we combine ( 3.25 ) and ( 3.26 ). Choosing $\varepsilon_{m}$ sufficiently small, e.g. $\varepsilon_{m}=\frac{r-1}{4C_{m}}$ , where $C_{m}$ is as in ( 3.25 ),
multiplying ( 3.25 ) with $\varepsilon_{m}$ and then adding to ( 3.26 ), we deduce ( 3.13a ) for $\lambda>0$ sufficiently small which is independent of $m$ , e.g. $\lambda=\frac{r-1}{4}$ . 3.2.2. Estimates for ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ For ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ , the key step is to establish the weighted $L^{2}$ estimate, i.e.  the bound ( 3.13b ) for $m=0$ .
For a radially symmetric weight $\varphi_{A}$ which is to be chosen,
using that $\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}=\mathbf{A}\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}$ (see ( 1.5 )) and integrating by parts, we obtain $\int{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{A}\varphi_{A}dy=-\int\Big(\frac{1}{2}(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla|\mathbf{A}|^{2}+\Bigl(r-1+\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}\Bigl)|\mathbf{A}|^{2}\Big)\varphi_{A}=-\int D_{A}(\varphi_{A})|\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{A},$ where we have defined the damping term $D_{A}(\varphi_{A})\triangleq-\frac{\nabla\cdot((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\varphi_{A})}{2\varphi_{A}}+\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}+(r-1).$ Choice of weights Our goal is to construct $\varphi_{A}$ such that the above defined damping term satisfies (3.27) $D_{A}(\varphi_{A})(\xi)\geq\tilde{\lambda}>0,\qquad\forall\xi>0,$ for some $\tilde{\lambda}>0$ . We will achieve this by designing $\varphi_{A}$ such that (3.28a) $\displaystyle\varphi_{A}=\xi^{-\beta_{1}}g(\xi)^{\beta_{2}}(1+\beta_{3}\langle\xi\rangle)^{\beta_{1}-2-\kappa_{1}},\ \beta_{1}\in(3,4),\beta_{2}\gg 1,0<\beta_{3}\ll 1,$ (3.28b) $\displaystyle\varphi_{A}\asymp\xi^{-\beta_{1}}\langle\xi\rangle^{\beta_{1}-2-\kappa_{1}},\quad|\nabla\varphi_{A}|\lesssim\xi^{-1}\varphi_{A},$ for a radial weight $g\asymp 1$ (to be chosen), where $\kappa_{1}$ is chosen in ( 3.15 ). Since $\mathbf{A}(0)=0$ , for any sufficiently smooth $\mathbf{A}$ , we have that $|\mathbf{A}^{2}|\varphi_{A}$ is locally integrable near $y=0\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ when $\beta_{1}<4$ . We use the singular weight $\xi^{-\beta_{1}}$ to extract the damping effect of the transport operator $(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla$ ; a direct calculation yields $D_{A}(\varphi_{A})=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{A}}{\varphi_{A}}-\frac{1}{2}(2+\operatorname{div}(\bar{\mathbf{U}}))+\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}+(r-1).$ Recalling that $\nabla\cdot\bar{\mathbf{U}}=\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}+\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}$ , and using the formula for $\varphi_{A}$ in ( 3.28 ), we further obtain $\displaystyle D_{A}(\varphi_{A})$ $\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{1}}{2}\frac{\xi+\bar{U}}{\xi}-\frac{\beta_{2}}{2}\frac{(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}g}{g}-\frac{\beta_{1}-2-\kappa_{1}}{2}\frac{(\xi+\bar{U})\beta_{3}\xi}{(1+\beta_{3}\langle\xi\rangle)\langle\xi\rangle}+\Bigl(r-2-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{U}}{\xi}\Bigr)$ $\displaystyle\triangleq I_{1}(\xi)+I_{2}(\xi)+I_{3}(\xi)+I_{4}(\xi).$ We will choose $4-\beta_{1}>0,\beta_{3}>0$ small, $\beta_{2}>0$ large, and $\partial_{\xi}g\leq 0$ with $g\asymp 1$ . Using ( 2.4a ) and ( 2.4e ), $\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\bigl(I_{1}+I_{4}\bigr)(\xi)=\tfrac{\beta_{1}}{2}(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0))+(r-2)=(2\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)+r)-(2-\tfrac{\beta_{1}}{2})(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)),$ $\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\bigl(I_{1}+I_{4}\bigr)(\xi)=\tfrac{\beta_{1}}{2}+(r-2).$ Using that $r>1$ , $2\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)+r>0$ (see ( 2.4e )), and $1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)>0$ (see ( 2.4b )), we can choose $\beta_{1}\in(3,4)$ close to $4$ such that $(2\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0)+r)-\frac{1}{2}(4-\beta_{1})(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}(0))\geq 2c_{1}$ for some $0<c_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}$ (which only depends on $\bar{U}$ and $r$ ). Therefore, by also appealing to $\partial_{\xi}g\leq 0$ , $\xi+\bar{U}>0$ (see ( 2.4d )), and to the continuity of $I_{i}(\xi)$ in $\xi$ , there exist $0<p<q$ such that $I_{1}(\xi)+I_{4}(\xi)+I_{2}(\xi)\geq I_{1}(\xi)+I_{4}(\xi)\geq c_{1}>0,\quad\xi\in[0,p]\cup[q,\infty).$ Next, we choose a smooth and radially symmetric function $g$ such that $g(\xi)=1,\xi\leq\tfrac{p}{2},\quad g(\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2},\xi\geq 2q,\quad\partial_{\xi}g\leq 0,\xi>0,\quad\partial_{\xi}g\leq-c_{2},\quad\xi\in[p,q].$ for some $c_{2}>0$ which only depends on $p$ and $q$ . Since ( 2.4d ) implies that $\xi+\bar{U}>c_{3}\mathbf{1}_{[p,q]}(\xi)$ for $c_{3}=p\kappa>0$ , choosing $\beta_{2}$ large enough we may ensure that $I_{1}(\xi)+I_{2}(\xi)+I_{4}(\xi)\geq\tfrac{\beta_{2}}{2}c_{2}c_{3}-\max_{\xi\in[p,q]}(|I_{1}(\xi)|+|I_{4}(\xi)|)>c_{1}>0,\quad\xi\in[p,q].$ Thus, we have shown that by choosing the function $g$ and the parameters $\beta_{1}$ and $\beta_{2}$ , we may ensure that $I_{1}(\xi)+I_{2}(\xi)+I_{4}(\xi)>c_{1}>0$ for all $\xi>0$ , for some $c_{1}>0$ . Finally, we handle $I_{3}$ . Note that the remaining free parameter in the definition ( 3.28 ) of $\varphi_{A}$ is $\beta_{3}$ , which only enters the definition of $I_{3}$ . Since $I_{2}\geq 0$ and $\beta_{1}-2-\kappa_{1}>0$ , using the asymptotics ( 2.4a ), the bound $\frac{\beta_{3}\xi}{(1+\beta_{3}\langle\xi\rangle)\langle\xi\rangle}\leq\frac{1}{\xi}$ , for $\xi\gg 1$ we have $D_{A}\geq I_{1}+I_{3}+I_{4}\geq\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{1}}{2}-\frac{\beta_{1}-\kappa_{1}-2}{2}\Bigr)\frac{\xi+\bar{U}}{\xi}+(r-2)-C\langle\xi\rangle^{-r}\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}+2}{2}+(r-2)-C\langle\xi\rangle^{-r}.$ By also appealing to the previously established lower bound for $I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{4}$ , for $\xi\lesssim 1$ we have $D_{A}\geq I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{4}-C\beta_{3}\xi\geq c_{1}-C\beta_{3}\xi.$ Since $\kappa_{1}>0$ and $r-1>0$ , the first of the two lower bounds for $D_{A}$ above
imply that there exists $q_{2}>0$ sufficiently large such that $D_{A}\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{4}=\frac{1}{16}$ for all $\xi\geq q_{2}$ . On the other hand, taking $\beta_{3}>0$ small enough to ensure that $\frac{c_{1}}{2}\geq C\beta_{3}q_{2}$ , from the second of the two lower bounds for $D_{A}$ above, we obtain $D_{A}\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}$ for all $\xi\in(0,q_{2})$ . Together, these bounds establish ( 3.27 ), for $\tilde{\lambda}=\min\{\frac{1}{16},\frac{c_{1}}{2}\}>0$ . We also note that the properties of $\varphi_{A}$ stated in ( 3.28b ) follow from the definitions of $g,\varphi_{A}$ . Having established ( 3.27 ), we obtain the desired weighted $L^{2}$ stability estimate (3.29) $\int{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{A}\varphi_{A}\leq-\tilde{\lambda}\int|\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{A}.$ Weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates The weighted $H^{2m}$ estimates with weights $\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}$ are similar to those for ${\mathcal{L}}$ in Section 3.2.1 .
In analogy to the derivation in ( 3.16 ), we may apply $\Delta^{m}$ to ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ (as defined in ( 2.6e )) and then decompose the resulting expression into a transport, damping, stretching, and remainder term; to avoid redundancy we do not spell out these details. Analogously to the bounds ( 3.21 ),
