( 3.23 ), ( 3.24 ), and recalling ( 3.20 ), we obtain $\int\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{A}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}dy\leq\int D_{A,2m}|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+C_{m}\int|\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{g},$ where $D_{A,2m}=2m(\xi+\bar{U})\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}-(r-1)-2m(1+\partial_{\xi}\bar{U}).$ Compared to ( 3.21 ), ( 3.23 ), in the above estimates, we do not need to estimate cross terms ( 3.19 ), ( 3.22 ), which contribute to the bounds $\alpha\bar{\Sigma}|\frac{\partial_{\xi}\varphi_{1}}{\varphi_{1}}|$ in ( 3.21 ) and $2m\alpha|\partial_{\xi}\bar{\Sigma}|$ in ( 3.23 ).
Using Lemma 3.1 with $\ell=0$ , and choosing $\tilde{m}_{0}$ large enough, for $m\geq\tilde{m}_{0}$ , we obtain $D_{A,2m}\leq-(r-1)-2m\mu_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}+C\langle\xi\rangle^{-\kappa_{3}}\leq-(r-1),$ uniformly in $\xi>0$ . Since $\varphi_{g}\lesssim\varphi_{A}$ , combining the above estimates and ( 3.29 ), and then choosing $\varepsilon_{m}$ small enough, we obtain $\displaystyle\int\Bigl(\varepsilon_{m}\Delta^{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\Delta^{m}\mathbf{A}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{A}\varphi_{A}\Bigr)dy\leq-\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\int\Bigl(\varepsilon_{m}|\Delta^{m}\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{2m}^{2}\varphi_{g}+|\mathbf{A}|^{2}\varphi_{A}\Bigr)dy$ for some $\tilde{\lambda}_{1}>0$ independent of $m$ .
This proves ( 3.13b ). Clearly, by choosing $\lambda$ smaller, $m_{0}$ larger, and $\varepsilon_{m}$ smaller, we can obtain the coercive estimates ( 3.13a ), ( 3.13b ) for the same set of parameters. This concludes the proof of Theorem 3.2 . 3.3. Compact perturbation via Riesz representation In this section, using the estimates established earlier in Theorem 3.2 , we construct a compact operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ such that ${\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is dissipative in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . For this purpose we fix $m_{0}\geq 6$ . We also recall from Lemma 3.3 that the Hilbert spaces $\{{\mathcal{X}}^{i}\}_{i\geq 0}$ are nested, with ${\mathcal{X}}^{i+1}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{i}\subset\ldots\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ for all $i\geq 0$ ; we will implicitly use this fact throughout this section. Proposition 3.4 . For any $m\geq m_{0}$ , there exists a bounded linear operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ with: (a) for any $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ we have ${\rm supp}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)\subset B(0,4R_{4}),$ where $R_{4}$ is chosen in Theorem 3.2 (in particular, it is independent of $m$ ); (b) the operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is compact from ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ ; (c) the enhanced smoothing property ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ holds; (d) the operator ${\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is dissipative on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ and we have the estimate (3.30) $\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq-\lambda\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ for all $f\in\{(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\colon{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\}$ , ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{S})$ , and where $\lambda>0$ is the parameter from ( 3.13a ) (in particular, it is independent of $m$ ). Item (d) shows that ${\mathcal{L}}$ is a compact perturbation of the dissipative operator ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}\triangleq{\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , for any $m\geq m_{0}$ .
By further regularizing ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ one could define ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{\infty}=\cap_{m\geq 0}{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , but we do not need this extra regularity and thus do not seek this refinement; we only need the ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ -regularity claimed in item (c) in order to simplify the nonlinear estimates in Section 4.4.2 . Proof. Fix $R_{4}$ as in Theorem 3.2 . We let $0\leq\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\leq 1$ be smooth cutoff functions such that (3.31a) $\displaystyle\chi_{1}(y)=1,\ |y|\leq 2R_{4},\quad\chi_{1}(y)=0,\ |y|\geq 4R_{4},$ (3.31b) $\displaystyle\chi_{2}(y)=1,\ |y|\leq 4R_{4},\quad\chi_{2}(y)=0,\ |y|>8R_{4}.$ Throughout this proof we also fix $m\geq m_{0}$ , where $m_{0}\geq 6$ is given by Theorem 3.2 . Riesz representation We consider the bilinear form $B_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathbb{R}}$ defined by $B_{m}(f,g)\triangleq{\sum}_{1\leq i\leq 3}\int\chi_{1}f_{i}\,g_{i},$ for $f=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)=(U_{1},U_{2},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ and $g=(\mathbf{U}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})=(U^{\prime}_{1},U^{\prime}_{2},\Sigma^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . We note that for $\varphi_{g}$ as defined in ( 3.15 ), we have that $\langle 4R_{4}\rangle^{-\kappa_{1}-2}\leq\varphi_{g}\leq 1$ on ${\rm supp}(\chi_{1})$ and hence
by also recalling the nested property ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ (cf. Lemma 3.3 ) we have the trivial estimate (3.32) $|B_{m}(f,g)|\lesssim\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ Here and throughout the proof the implicit constants in $\lesssim$ are allowed to depend on $R_{4}$ . It is clear from the above bound that $B_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)$ is well defined on the larger domain ${\mathcal{X}}^{0}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ , but we shall not use this fact. For each fixed $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ , since $B_{m}(f,\cdot)$ is a bounded linear functional on the Hilbert space ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , using the Riesz representation theorem, there exists a unique ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ such that $\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=B_{m}(f,g),\quad\forall g\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}.$ Using ( 3.32 ) and duality, we immediately obtain (3.33) $\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=\sup_{\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq 1}\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=\sup_{\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq 1}B_{m}(f,g)\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}.$ Since $B_{m}(f,g)$ is linear in $f$ , from ( 3.33 ) we deduce that ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is a bounded linear operator from ${\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ to ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Proof of item (a) For any $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ , from the definitions of $\chi_{1}$ and $B_{m}$ , we deduce that $\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=0$ for all $g\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ such that $\mathrm{supp}(g)\subseteq\{y:|y|\geq 4R_{4}\}$ . By the definition of the inner-product on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ (it is equivalent to the $H^{2m}$ inner-product on compact subsets), it follows that $\mathrm{supp}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)\subset B(0,4R_{4})$ . Moreover, with $\chi_{2}$ as defined in ( 3.31 ) we have $B_{m}(f,\cdot)=B_{m}(\chi_{2}f,\cdot)$ and hence ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f={\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\chi_{2}f)$ . With these two facts, it also follows that $\chi_{2}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f={\mathcal{K}}_{m}f$ . Proof of item (b) Since $\varphi_{2m}\asymp_{m,\Omega}1$ and $\varphi_{g}\asymp_{\Omega}1$ on any compact set $\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}$ (in particular, for $\Omega=\overline{B(0,4R_{4})}$ ), it follows that for any function $h$ such that ${\rm supp}(h)\subset\Omega$ , we have $\|h\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\asymp_{m,\Omega}\|h\|_{H^{2m}(\Omega)}$ ; this norm equivalence will be used several times throughout the remainder of the proof. To establish the compactness of ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , we note that if $\{f_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}$ is a bounded sequence in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , then using $\|\chi_{2}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ for any $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , and appealing to the above discussed norm equivalence, we obtain that $\{\chi_{2}f_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}$ is a bounded sequence of functions in $H^{2m}(B(0,8R_{4}))$ . Using the Rellich-Kondrachov compact embedding theorem, there exists a subsequence $\{\chi_{2}f_{k_{j}}\}_{j\geq 1}$ which is convergent in $L^{2}(B(0,8R_{4}))$ , and a-posteriori in ${\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ . By the continuity of ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ it follows that $\{{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\chi_{2}f_{k_{j}})\}_{j\geq 1}$ is convergent in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , and since ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\chi_{2}f_{k_{j}})={\mathcal{K}}_{m}(f_{k_{j}})$ we deduce the desired pre-compactness of the sequence $\{{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(f_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}$ in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . We obtain that ${\mathcal{K}}$ is a bounded compact operator from ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Proof of item (c) We fix $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ . Using the definition of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ in ( 3.14 ), the fact that $\varphi_{2m}\asymp_{m}1$ and $\varphi_{g}\asymp 1$ on $B(0,4R_{4})$ , the middle inequality in ( 3.32 ), the inclusion in Lemma 3.3 , the bound ( 3.33 ), and the compact support property of ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ established in item (a), we deduce $\displaystyle\Bigl|\int\Delta^{m}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)\cdot\Delta^{m}g\Bigr|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\bigl|\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\big|+\bigl|\langle{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\big|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}+\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}$ (3.34) $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}+\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}.$ for any $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}$ and $g\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . We would like to set $g=\Delta^{m}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)$ in ( 3.34 ), but this is not immediately possible since we only know ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , which provides insufficient regularity. As such, we need to regularize ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f$ .
We choose a radially symmetric non-negative function $\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}$ with $\int\phi=1$ and define $\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)=\varepsilon^{-2}\phi(y/\varepsilon)$ .
Let $F\triangleq\Delta^{m}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f),\quad G_{\varepsilon}\triangleq\phi_{\varepsilon}\ast(\phi_{\varepsilon}\ast F).$ Using the equivalence of the norms $\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ and $\|\cdot\|_{H^{2m}}$ for functions which have compact support in $B(0,4R_{4})$ , from the fact that ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ and item (a) we deduce that $F\in L^{2}$ has compact support, and $G_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Inserting $g\mapsto G_{\varepsilon}$ in ( 3.34 ), upon integrating by parts we deduce that $\displaystyle\|\nabla^{m}(\phi_{\varepsilon}\ast F)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\leq_{m}\Bigl|\int F\cdot\Delta^{m}(\phi_{\varepsilon}\ast(\phi_{\varepsilon}\ast F))\Bigr|$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|G_{\varepsilon}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}.$ Passing $\varepsilon\to 0$ , we deduce that $\nabla^{m}F\in L^{2}$ and $\|\nabla^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}$ .
Since ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}f$ has compact support, we thus obtain $\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{\lfloor 3m/2\rfloor}}\lesssim_{m}\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{\dot{H}^{3m}}\lesssim_{m}\int|\nabla^{m}\Delta^{m}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)|=\|\nabla^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}.$ Since $m\geq 6$ , using the nested property from Lemma 3.3 we deduce the boundedness ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ . Proof of item (d) Using the definitions of $B_{m}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ , the estimate ( 3.13a ), recalling that $\varphi_{g}\leq 1$ , and letting $\bar{C}>0$ be exactly as in ( 3.13a ), we deduce $\langle({\mathcal{L}}-\bar{C}{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq-\lambda\langle f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\bar{C}\int(\mathbf{1}_{|y|\leq R_{4}}-\chi_{1})|f|^{2}dx\leq-\lambda\langle f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},$ where $f=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma),{\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{S})$ . Upon re-defining ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ to equal $\bar{C}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ , which does not alter the proofs of items (a)–(c), we deduce the dissippativity claimed in ( 3.30 ). MM $\square$ 3.4. Construction of the semigroup For any $m\geq m_{0}$ we construct the strongly continuous semigroups $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m},\quad e^{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}s}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m},\quad{\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})={\mathcal{D}}_{m}+{\mathcal{K}}_{m}.$ by directly solving the associated linear PDEs. Here ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is as constructed in Proposition 3.4 .
In this section, without loss of generality we consider semigroups defined for $s\geq 0$ , although in applications for Section 4 the interval $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ is the relevant one. 3.4.1. From linear PDEs to semigroups The relation between linear evolutionary PDEs and semigroups is classical; see e.g. [ 37 ] . Following Definition 6.1, Chapter II of [ 37 ] , we consider the abstract initial value problem for a linear operator ${\mathcal{A}}:D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X\to X$ with domain $D({\mathcal{A}})$ : (3.35) $\tfrac{d}{dt}u(t)={\mathcal{A}}u(t),\quad t\geq 0,\quad u(0)=x\in X,$ where $X$ is a Banach space. A function $u:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to X$ is a (classical) solution of ( 3.35 ) if $u$ is continuously differentiable with respect to $X$ , $u(t)\in D({\mathcal{A}})$ for all $t\geq 0$ , and ( 3.35 ) holds in $X$ for all $t>0$ . We use the notation $u(x,t)$ to denote a solution $u$ from initial data $x\in X$ , at time $t$ . We recall: Theorem 3.5 (Theorem 6.7, Chapter II, [ 37 ] ) . Let ${\mathcal{A}}:D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X\to X$ be a closed operator. The following properties are equivalent: (a) ${\mathcal{A}}$ generates a strongly continuous semigroup. (b) For every $x\in D({\mathcal{A}})$ , there exists a unique solution $u(x,\cdot)$ of the initial value problem ( 3.35 ). The operator ${\mathcal{A}}$ has dense domain, and for every sequence $\{x_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset D({\mathcal{A}})$ satisfying $\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=0$ , one has $\lim_{n\to\infty}u(x_{n},t)=0$ uniformly on compact intervals $[0,t_{0}]$ . The above theorem states that ${\mathcal{A}}$ generates a strongly continuous semi-group if and only if the linear PDEs is well-posed: ( 3.35 ) has a unique solution, and satisfies continuous dependence on the data. 3.4.2. Solving linear PDEs We define the domains of the operators ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})$ and ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}={\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ as (3.36) $D({\mathcal{D}}_{m})=D({\mathcal{L}})\triangleq\{(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m},{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\}.$ Recall from ( 2.6d ) and ( 2.6f ) that the linearized operator ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})$ is defined via (3.37a) $\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ $\displaystyle=-(r-1)\mathbf{U}-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}-\alpha\Sigma\nabla\bar{\Sigma}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\nabla\Sigma,$ (3.37b) $\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ $\displaystyle=-(r-1)\Sigma-(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\Sigma-\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\Sigma}-\alpha\operatorname{div}(\bar{\mathbf{U}})\Sigma-\alpha\operatorname{div}(\mathbf{U})\bar{\Sigma},$ resulting in the linearized versions of the evolution equations ( 2.6a ) and ( 2.6c ) (3.37c) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ $\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)=\bigl({\mathcal{L}}_{U}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma),{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\bigr).$ Note that ${\mathcal{L}}$ does not involve $A$ , so that ( 3.37 ) is a closed evolution for $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ , which is why we drop the dependence on $A$ .
We also emphasize that the linear operator ${\mathcal{L}}$ preserves radial symmetry (recall ( 1.5 )), and so for initial data in $D({\mathcal{L}})\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , we may expect the solution of ( 3.37c ) to lie in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Denote $\mathbf{V}=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)=(U_{1},U_{2},\Sigma)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ . The linearized equations ( 3.37c ) may be written in the form of a symmetric hyperbolic system (3.38) $\partial_{s}\mathbf{V}+B_{1}(y)\partial_{1}\mathbf{V}+B_{2}(y)\partial_{2}\mathbf{V}=M(y)\,\mathbf{V},\quad B_{i}(y)=(y_{i}+\bar{U}_{i}(y)){\rm Id}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}(y)(E_{i3}+E_{3i}),$ where $E_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}$ is the matrix with $1$ at the $(i,j)$ entry and $0$ s at all other entries, and the matrix $M\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}$ depends only on the profiles $(\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma})$ . In particular, since the profiles $(\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma})$ are smooth, so are the coefficients $B_{i},M$ . For initial data $\mathbf{V}_{0}=\mathbf{V}|_{s=0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})$ the symmetric hyperbolic system ( 3.38 )
has a unique, global-in-time, smooth solution $\mathbf{V}$ , and $\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})$ ; this fact may for instance be established using the vanishing viscosity method (see e.g. [ 38 , Section 7.3.2] ), using the fact that the $B_{i}$ and $M$ are bounded with bounded derivatives on any compact domain, and using finite speed of propagation. 21 21 21 A much more general existence and uniqueness theorem is established in [ 50 ] . Next, we use approximation of the initial data to show that for $\mathbf{V}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ the solution $\mathbf{V}$ of ( 3.38 ) satisfies $\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ for all $s>0$ .
For any $\mathbf{V}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , there exists a radially symmetric $\mathbf{V}_{0}^{(n)}\in C_{c}^{\infty}$ such that $\|\mathbf{V}_{0}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\to 0$ as $n\to\infty$ . For initial datum $\mathbf{V}_{0}^{(n)}$ , we obtain a unique, global-in-time, radially symmetric smooth solution $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}$ such that $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)\in C_{c}^{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ for $s>0$ . Using that the evolution ( 3.38 ) is linear, we may apply the energy estimates in Theorem 3.2 (more precisely, the bound ( 3.13a )) to both $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}$ and $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}$ , to obtain $\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{ds}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\bar{C}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}\leq\bar{C}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{ds}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\bar{C}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}\leq\bar{C}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}$ to deduce (3.39) $\|(\mathbf{V}^{(n)}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})})(\cdot,s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{\bar{C}s}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}-\mathbf{V}^{(n^{\prime})}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},\qquad\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{\bar{C}s}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ Since $\{\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}\}_{n\geq 1}$ is a Cauchy sequence in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , from ( 3.39 ) we deduce $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)\to\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)$ in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ uniformly for $s\in[0,T]$ , for any $T>0$ . Moreover, ( 3.39 ) yields $\|\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{\bar{C}s}\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ . In order to show that $\|\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\to 0$ as $s\to 0$ , for $n\geq 1$ we use the decomposition $\displaystyle\|\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ $\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ (3.40) $\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+(e^{\bar{C}s}+1)\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ We thus only need to show that for any fixed $n\geq 1$ , we have $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)\to\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}$ in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ as $s\to 0$ . Since we already know that $\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})$ , with uniform compact support for $s\in[0,1]$ , we deduce that ${\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}([0,1];{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ , and so $\partial_{s}\mathbf{V}^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}((0,1);{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ . Thus, $\lim_{s\to 0}\|\mathbf{V}^{(n)}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}^{(n)}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=0$ . Finally, we first pass $s\to 0$ , and then $n\to\infty$ in ( 3.40 ), to deduce $\lim_{s\to 0}\|\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=0$ . From the energy estimates in Theorem 3.2 and estimates similar to ( 3.39 ), we also obtain the uniqueness of solutions to ( 3.38 ) (hence ( 3.37c )), from any initial data in ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Therefore, for any $\mathbf{V}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , we have constructed the solution operator $S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)$ for
( 3.37 ) satisfying (3.41a) $\displaystyle S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s):{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m},\quad S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(t)=S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s+t),\quad s,t\geq 0,$ (3.41b) $\displaystyle\|S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{\bar{C}s},\quad\lim_{s\to 0}\|S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)\mathbf{V}_{0}-\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}=0.$ The semigroup property follows from the uniqueness of the solution. The growth rate $\bar{C}$ is as in ( 3.13a ). In order to apply Theorem 3.5 , we need to verify condition (b). Consider $\mathbf{V}_{0}\in D({\mathcal{L}})$ , as defined in ( 3.36 ). Unique classical solvability requires that the solution $\mathbf{V}$ of ( 3.37 ) with initial data $\mathbf{V}_{0}$ , i.e. $\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)=S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)\mathbf{V}_{0}$ , satisfies $\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)\in D({\mathcal{L}})$ for $s>0$ . In order to prove this we observe that $[\partial_{s},{\mathcal{L}}]=0$ and hence $\partial_{s}(\partial_{s}\mathbf{V})={\mathcal{L}}(\partial_{s}\mathbf{V}),\quad(\partial_{s}\mathbf{V})|_{s=0}={\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{V}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}.$ Applying ( 3.41 ) to $\partial_{s}\mathbf{V}$ , we obtain ${\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)=\partial_{s}\mathbf{V}(\cdot,s)=S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)({\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{V}_{0})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , for all $s>0$ , as desired. This argument also shows that $\mathbf{V}\in{\rm Lip}([0,T],{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ .
The continuous dependence of data required in item (b) of Theorem 3.5 follows directly from the growth bound of $S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)$ , see ( 3.41 ). Concluding, we apply Theorem 3.5 with $({\mathcal{A}},X)=({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ , and obtain that ${\mathcal{L}}$ generates a strongly continuous semigroup $S_{{\mathcal{L}}}(s)=e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}$ , which satisfies the growth bound ( 3.41 ). 3.4.3. Semigroup for ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}$ In order to show that ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}={\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ generates a strongly continuous semigroup, we note that ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ is bounded (see Proposition 3.4 ), and so the Bounded Perturbation Theorem in [ 37 , Theorem 1.3, Chapter III] applies directly.
Using the coercive estimates ( 3.30 ), we moreover obtain (3.42) $e^{s{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m},\quad\|e^{s{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{-\lambda s}.$ The decay estimate of the semigroup ( 3.42 ) further implies the following spectral property of ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}$ (see [ 37 , Theorem 1.10, Chapter II] ) (3.43) $\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\lambda\}\subset\rho({\mathcal{D}}_{m}),$ where $\rho({\mathcal{A}})$ denotes the resolvent set of an operator ${\mathcal{A}}$ . Note that the estimates
( 3.42 ) and ( 3.43 ) apply to all $({\mathcal{D}}_{m},{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ for $m\geq m_{0}$ , with $\lambda$ independent of $m$ . 3.5. Decay estimates of $e^{{\mathcal{L}}t}$ In this section, we follow [ 37 ] to obtain decay estimates for the semigroup $e^{{\mathcal{L}}t}$ . For a semigroup $e^{{\mathcal{A}}t}:X\to X$ on a Banach space $X$ denote by ${\sigma}({\mathcal{A}})$ the spectrum of ${\mathcal{A}}$ , i.e., the set $\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:z-{\mathcal{A}}\mbox{ is not bijective}\}$ . We also introduce the spectral bound ${\mathsf{s}}({\mathcal{A}})$ , the growth bound $\omega_{0}({\mathcal{A}})$ , and the essential growth bound $\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{A}})$ , defined by $\displaystyle{\mathsf{s}}({\mathcal{A}})$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\sup\bigl\{\mathrm{Re}(z)\colon z\in\sigma({\mathcal{A}})\bigr\},$ $\displaystyle\omega_{0}({\mathcal{A}})$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\inf\bigl\{\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mbox{ there exists }M_{\omega}\geq 1\mbox{ such that }\|e^{{\mathcal{A}}t}\|_{X\to X}\leq M_{\omega}e^{\omega t}\mbox{ for all }t\geq 0\bigr\},$ $\displaystyle\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{A}})$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\inf_{t>0}\tfrac{1}{t}\log\|e^{{\mathcal{A}}t}\|_{ess},$ where the norm $\|\cdot\|_{ess}$ is defined as $\|T\|_{ess}=\inf_{K\colon X\to X\mathrm{\ is\ compact}}\|T-K\|_{X\to X}.$ With this notation, we have the following result: Proposition 3.6 (Corollary 2.11, Chapter IV, [ 37 ] ) . Let $e^{{\mathcal{A}}t}$ be a strongly continuous semigroup generated by ${\mathcal{A}}\colon D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X\to X$ , a closed operator. Then $\omega_{0}({\mathcal{A}})=\max\{\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{A}}),{\mathsf{s}}({\mathcal{A}})\}.$ Moreover, for every $\omega>\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{A}})$ , the set $\sigma_{c}=\sigma({\mathcal{A}})\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathrm{Re}(z)\geq\omega\}$ is finite, and the corresponding spectral projection has finite rank. We apply Proposition 3.6 to $({\mathcal{A}},X)=({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ , where ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma})={\mathcal{D}}_{m}+{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ is the compact operator constructed in Proposition 3.4 . From the decay estimate ( 3.42 ) we obtain $\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{D}}_{m})\leq-\lambda$ . Since $\omega_{ess}$ is invariant under compact perturbations (see [ 37 , Proposition 2.12, Chapter IV] ), we get (3.44) $\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{L}})=\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{D}}_{m})\leq-\lambda.$ 3.5.1. Hyperbolic decomposition In this section, based on the estimate of $\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{L}})$ from ( 3.44 ),
we orthogonally decompose the space ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ (see ( 3.45 ) below) and obtain decay estimates on $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}$ for the stable part, and growth estimates for the unstable part.
For this purpose, we first recall the following spectral decomposition for a closed operator ${\mathcal{A}}$ , based on Riesz projections. Proposition 3.7 (Theorem 2.1, Chapter XV, Part IV, page 326, [ 41 ] ) . Suppose ${\mathcal{A}}:D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X\to X$ is a closed operator with specturm $\sigma({\mathcal{A}})=\sigma\cup\tau$ , where $\sigma$ is contained in a bounded Cauchy domain $\Delta$ such that $\bar{\Delta}\cap\tau=\emptyset$ . Let $\Gamma$ be the (oriented) boundary of $\Delta$ . Then: (i) $P_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma}(z-{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}dz$ is a projection, (ii) the subspaces $M=\mathrm{Rg}\,P_{\sigma}$ (the image of $P_{\sigma}$ ) and $N=\ker P_{\sigma}$ are ${\mathcal{A}}-$ invariant, (iii) the subspace $M$ is contained in $D({\mathcal{A}})$ and ${\mathcal{A}}|_{M}$ is bounded, (iv) $\sigma({\mathcal{A}}|_{M})=\sigma$ and $\sigma({\mathcal{A}}|_{N})=\tau$ . Our goal is to apply the above result to $({\mathcal{A}},X)=({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{X}}^{m})$ .
From ( 3.44 ) and Proposition 3.6 , we have that $\sigma_{\eta}\triangleq\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\eta\},\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad\eta\triangleq\tfrac{4}{5}\lambda<\lambda,$ only consists of finitely many eigenvalues of ${\mathcal{L}}$ , with finite multiplicity. Without loss of generality 22 22 22 The rigorous argument would be as follows. For $\bar{\eta}=\frac{9}{10}\lambda$ , we have that $\sigma_{\bar{\eta}}=\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\bar{\eta}\}$ only consists of finitely many eigenvalues of ${\mathcal{L}}$ , with finite multiplicity. As such, for a.e. $\theta\in(\frac{7}{10},\frac{9}{10})$ the line in the complex plane with real-abscisa $-\theta\lambda$ does not intersect $\sigma({\mathcal{L}})$ . We have assumed without loss of generality that this $\theta$ may be taken to equal $\frac{4}{5}$ , but of course the below arguments all hold as is if we replace $\eta=\frac{4}{5}\lambda$ with $\eta=\theta\lambda$ for some $\theta\in(\frac{7}{10},\frac{9}{10})$ . we have that $\sigma_{\eta}$ is isolated from $\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\backslash\sigma_{\eta}$ . Applying Proposition 3.7 with ${\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{L}}$ , $X={\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , and $\tau=\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\backslash\sigma_{\eta}$ , we obtain the hyperbolic decomposition (3.45) ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}={\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}\oplus{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}},\quad\sigma({\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}})=\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\backslash\sigma_{\eta}\subset\{z:\mathrm{Re}(z)\leq-\eta\},\quad\sigma({\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}})=\sigma_{\eta}.$ Moreover, from Proposition 3.6 it follows that we can decompose the unstable part as (3.46) ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}=\bigoplus_{z\in\sigma_{\eta}}\ker((z-{\mathcal{L}})^{\mu_{z}}),\quad\mu_{z}<\infty,\quad|\sigma_{\eta}|<+\infty.$ We will also show in Section 3.6 that ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}$ is spanned by smooth functions. Since ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}$ is ${\mathcal{L}}$ invariant, the restriction of the semigroup $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}$ on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}$ is generated by ${\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}}$ . Moreover, since $\max(\omega_{ess}({\mathcal{L}}),{\mathsf{s}}(\sigma({\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}})))\leq-\eta$ , from Proposition 3.6 and the definition of $\omega_{0}({\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}})$ we obtain (3.47) $\|e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq C_{m}e^{-s\eta_{\mathsf{s}}}\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},\quad\forall V_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}},\quad s>0,\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad\eta_{\mathsf{s}}\triangleq\tfrac{3}{5}\lambda<\tfrac{4}{5}\lambda=\eta.$ We also note that since ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}$ has finite dimension, $\sigma({\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}})=\sigma_{\eta}$ , the operator ${\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}}}$ may be represented as a matrix with eigenvalues $>-\eta$ ; therefore, we obtain (3.48) $\|e^{-{\mathcal{L}}s}\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq C_{m}e^{\eta s}\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.\quad\forall\mathbf{V}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{u}},\quad s>0.$ 3.5.2. Additional decay estimates of ${\mathcal{L}}$ In order to localize the initial data (see ( 4.4a ) below), we will need the following decay estimates for the linear evolution ( 3.37c ), with initial data supported in the far-field. Proposition 3.8 . Let $R_{4}$ be as defined in Theorem 3.2 . Consider ( 3.37 ) with initial data $\mathbf{V}_{0}=(\mathbf{U}_{0},\Sigma_{0})$ with $\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{U}_{0})\cup\mathrm{supp}(\Sigma_{0})\subset B(0,R)^{\complement}$ for some $R>4R_{4}>\xi_{s}$ .
For any $m\geq m_{0}$ , the solution $\mathbf{V}(s)=(\mathbf{U}(s),\Sigma(s))=e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}\mathbf{V}_{0}$ satisfies $\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{V}(s))\subset B(0,4R_{4})^{\complement},\qquad\|e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq e^{-\lambda s}\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ Proof. The proof of Proposition 3.8 uses that the support of the solution $e^{{\mathcal{L}}s}\mathbf{V}_{0}$ is moving away from $y=0$ , remaining outside of $B(0,4R_{4})$ for all time. Since $\mathrm{supp}({\mathcal{K}}_{m}f)\subset B(0,4R_{4})$ (item (a) in Proposition 3.4 ), we
get ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}\mathbf{V}(s)=0$ for all time $s$ , and the desired decay estimate follows from ( 3.30 ) or ( 3.42 ). Based on the above discussion, we only need to show that the solution $\mathbf{V}(y,s)=0$ for all $y\in B(0,4R_{4})$ and $s>0$ . Let $\chi$ be a radially symmetric cutoff function with $\chi(y)=1$ for $|y|\leq 4R_{4}$ , $\chi(y)=0$ for $|y|\geq R>4R_{4}$ , and with $\chi(|y|)$ decreasing in $|y|$ . Our goal is to show that the weighted $L^{2}$ norm $\int(|\mathbf{U}(s)|^{2}+|\Sigma(s)|^{2})\chi$ of the solution $\mathbf{V}=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ of ( 3.37 ), vanishes identically for $s\geq 0$ . By assumption, we have that $\int(|\mathbf{U}(0)|^{2}+|\Sigma(0)|^{2})\chi=0$ , so it remains to compute $\frac{d}{ds}$ of this weighted $L^{2}$ norm using ( 3.37 ). Recall the decomposition of ${\mathcal{L}}$ from ( 3.16 ) with $m=0$ (hence, with ${\mathcal{R}}_{U,m}={\mathcal{R}}_{\Sigma,m}=0$ ). Performing weighted $L^{2}$ estimates analogous to the ones in the proof of Theorem 3.2 , for the transport terms we obtain $\displaystyle\int({\mathcal{T}}_{U}\cdot\mathbf{U}+{\mathcal{T}}_{\Sigma}\,\Sigma)\chi$ $\displaystyle\quad=-\int\Big((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{U}|^{2}\bigr)+(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}\bigr)+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\,\nabla\Sigma\cdot\mathbf{U}+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}\operatorname{div}(\mathbf{U})\Sigma\Big)\chi$ $\displaystyle\quad=\int\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{div}\Big((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\chi\Big)(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})+\alpha\nabla(\bar{\Sigma}\chi)\cdot\mathbf{U}\Sigma$ (3.49) $\displaystyle\quad\leq\int\tfrac{1}{2}\Big((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\chi+\chi\operatorname{div}(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\Big)(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})+\alpha\bigl(\bar{\Sigma}|\nabla\chi|+\chi|\nabla\bar{\Sigma}|\bigr)|\mathbf{U}\Sigma|.$ We focus on the terms in ( 3.49 ) involving $|\nabla\chi|$ . Since $\chi$ is radially symmetric, we get $(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\chi=(\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\chi$ . Using Cauchy-Schwarz, the fact that $\partial_{\xi}\chi(y)=0,|y|\leq 4R_{4}$ and $\partial_{\xi}\chi\leq 0$ globally, and using that ( 2.4c ) yields $\xi+\bar{U}(\xi)-\alpha\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)>0$ for $\xi=|y|>\xi_{s}$ (hence for $\xi>4R_{4}$ ), we obtain $\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl((y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\nabla\chi\bigr)(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}|\nabla\chi||\mathbf{U}\Sigma|$ $\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl((\xi+\bar{U})\partial_{\xi}\chi+\alpha\bar{\Sigma}|\partial_{\xi}\chi|\bigr)(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})$ $\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi+\bar{U}-\alpha\bar{\Sigma})\partial_{\xi}\chi(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})\leq 0.$ For remaining contributions, resulting from the $\chi$ -terms in ( 3.49 ), and from the ${\mathcal{D}}_{U},{\mathcal{S}}_{U},{\mathcal{D}}_{\Sigma},{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}$ -terms in ( 3.16 ), in light of ( 2.4a ) we have that $\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\chi\operatorname{div}(y+\bar{\mathbf{U}})(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})+\alpha\chi|\nabla\bar{\Sigma}||\mathbf{U}\Sigma|-(r-1)\chi(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})-\chi(\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\mathbf{U}}\cdot\mathbf{U}+\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\bar{\Sigma}\,\Sigma)$ $\displaystyle\leq C\chi(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})$ for some sufficiently large $C>0$ (depending on $\alpha,r,\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma}$ ). Thus, we obtain $\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{ds}\int(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})\chi=\int({\mathcal{L}}_{U}\cdot\mathbf{U}+{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}\,\Sigma)\chi\leq C\int(|\mathbf{U}|^{2}+\Sigma^{2})\chi,$ which implies via Grönwall that $\int(|\mathbf{U}(s)|^{2}+\Sigma(s)^{2})\chi=0$ for all $s\geq 0$ . The claim follows. MM $\square$ 3.6. Smoothness of unstable directions In this section, we show that the unstable space ${\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ given in ( 3.46 ) is spanned by smooth functions. This fact will be shown to follow from the following abstract lemma. Lemma 3.9 . Let $\{X^{i}\}_{i\geq 0}$ be a sequence of Banach spaces with $X^{i+1}\subset X^{i}$ for all $i\geq 0$ .
Assume that for any $i\geq i_{0}$ we can decompose the linear operator ${\mathcal{A}}\colon D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X^{i}\to X^{i}$ as ${\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{D}}_{i}+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}$ , where the linear operators ${\mathcal{D}}_{i}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}_{i}$ satisfy (3.50) ${\mathcal{D}}_{i}:D({\mathcal{A}})\subset X^{i}\to X^{i},\quad{\mathcal{K}}_{i}:X^{i-1}\to X^{i},\quad\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\lambda\}\subset\rho({\mathcal{D}}_{i}).$ Here, $\rho(\cdot)$ denotes the resolvent set of an operator and $\lambda>0$ is independent of $i\geq i_{0}$ . Fix $n\geq 0$ and $z\in{\mathbb{C}}$ with $\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\lambda$ . Assume that the functions $f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\in X^{i_{0}}$ satisfy $(z-{\mathcal{A}})f_{0}=0,\quad(z-{\mathcal{A}})f_{i}=f_{i-1},\quad\mbox{ for }\quad 1\leq i\leq n.$ Then, we have $f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\in X^{\infty}\triangleq\cap_{i\geq 0}X^{i}$ . Proof. It suffices to show that: $\mbox{if }f\in X^{i_{0}}\mbox{ satisfies }(z-{\mathcal{A}})f=F\in X^{\infty},\mbox{ then }f\in X^{\infty}.$ Indeed, the lemma then follows by induction on $i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}$ . In order to prove the above claim, we further use induction on $i\geq i_{0}$ to show that: (3.51) $\mbox{if }f\in X^{i_{0}}\cap X^{i-1}\mbox{ satisfies }(z-{\mathcal{A}})f=F\in X^{\infty},\mbox{ then }f\in X^{i}.$ The base case $i=i_{0}$ in ( 3.51 ) holds true automatically. For the inductive step, for $i-1\geq i_{0}$ we assume that $f\in X^{i-1}=X^{i_{0}}\cap X^{i-1}$ . Using that ${\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{D}}_{i}+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}_{i}:X^{i-1}\to X^{i}$ from ( 3.50 ), we get $(z-{\mathcal{D}}_{i})f=F+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}f\in X^{i}$ .
From ( 3.50 ) and the assumption $\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\lambda$ , we get $z\in\rho({\mathcal{D}}_{i})$ and thus $f=(z-{\mathcal{D}}_{i})^{-1}(F+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}f)\in X^{i}$ . This proves ( 3.51 ). By induction on $i$ , it follows that $f\in X^{\infty}$ . MM $\square$ We are now able to apply Lemma 3.9 with $({\mathcal{A}},\{X^{i}\}_{i\geq i_{0}})\rightsquigarrow({\mathcal{L}},\{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\}_{m\geq m_{0}})$ , and the decomposition ${\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{D}}_{m}+{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ for $m\geq m_{0}$ , with ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ as constructed in Proposition 3.4 , in order to describe the regularity of elements of $\ker((z-{\mathcal{L}})^{\mu_{z}})$ for $n\rightsquigarrow\mu_{z}<\infty$ , and $z\in\sigma_{\eta}$ (see ( 3.45 ) and ( 3.46 )). Indeed, the assumptions of Lemma 3.9 are satisfied in light of Lemma 3.3 , Proposition 3.4 , and ( 3.43 ). We fix $z\in\sigma_{\eta}=\sigma({\mathcal{L}})\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\mathrm{Re}(z)>-\eta\}$ , where we recall $-\eta>-\lambda$ . We also fix $g\in\ker((z-{\mathcal{L}})^{\mu_{z}})\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ for some integer $\mu_{z}<\infty$ , as constructed in ( 3.45 ) and ( 3.46 ).
We apply Lemma 3.9 with $f_{i}=(z-{\mathcal{L}})^{\mu_{z}-i}g$ for $0\leq i\leq\mu_{z}$ , to deduce that $\{f_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\mu_{z}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{\infty}\triangleq\cap_{m\geq m_{0}}{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ . Since ${\mathcal{X}}^{\infty}=\cap_{m\geq m_{0}}{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\subset C^{\infty},$ we deduce from ( 3.46 ) that ${\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ is spanned by smooth radially-symmetric functions. 4. Nonlinear stability The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1.1 , by constructing global solutions $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma,\mathbf{A})$ to ( 2.3 ), with a number of desirable properties, in the vicinity of the stationary profile $(\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma},0)$ . Throughout this section we fix $m\geq m_{0}$ , where $m_{0}$ is sufficiently large, as in Theorem 3.2 . 4.1. Decomposition of the solution We will use $\mathbf{V}=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma,\mathbf{A})$ to denote the nonlinear solution to ( 2.3 ). As in ( 2.5 ), we denote the perturbation to the stationary profile as $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\triangleq(\tilde{\mathbf{U}},\tilde{\Sigma},\mathbf{A})\triangleq(\mathbf{U}-\bar{\mathbf{U}},\Sigma-\bar{\Sigma},\mathbf{A}),$ and recall that this perturbation solves ( 2.6 )–( 2.7 ). We shall further decompose the perturbation 23 23 23 We emphasize that the $\tilde{\cdot}_{1}$ or $\tilde{\cdot}_{2}$ sub-index denote different parts of the perturbation, they do not represent Cartesian coordinates. as $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ , with $\tilde{\mathbf{U}}=\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2}$ , $\tilde{\Sigma}=\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}$ , so that (4.1) $\displaystyle\mathbf{V}=(\mathbf{U},\Sigma,\mathbf{A})=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}+\bar{\mathbf{V}},\quad\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A}),\quad\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{2},0),\quad\bar{\mathbf{V}}=(\bar{\mathbf{U}},\bar{\Sigma},0).$ The fields $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ and $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ are defined as the solutions of (4.2a) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}={\mathcal{L}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})+{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}),$ (4.2b) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}={\mathcal{L}}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2})+{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}),$ with initial data at time $s=s_{\mathsf{in}}=\log T^{-1/r}$ given by $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}$ as described in Theorem 4.1 , and with $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}$ as given by ( 4.4d ). The operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ in ( 4.2 ) is as defined in Proposition 3.4 ,
while the linear operators ${\mathcal{L}}$ , ${\mathcal{L}}_{V}$ , and the nonlinear term ${\mathcal{N}}_{V}$ are given in terms of ( 2.6 )–( 2.7 ) and ${\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}),\qquad{\mathcal{L}}_{V}=({\mathcal{L}}_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma},{\mathcal{L}}_{A}),\qquad{\mathcal{N}}_{V}=({\mathcal{N}}_{U},{\mathcal{N}}_{\Sigma},{\mathcal{N}}_{A}).$ It is clear, by definition, that a global solution $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ of ( 4.2 ) provides via ( 4.1 ) a global solution $\mathbf{V}$ of ( 2.3 ). In ( 4.2 ), and in the remainder of the section, we have abused notation two-fold. First, in ( 4.2a ) we note that the output of the ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ operator is an element of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ (hence, a $\mathbf{U}$ and a $\Sigma$ ) and so it does not contain an $\mathbf{A}$ -component (as is required by the $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ representation in ( 4.1 )); as such, in ( 4.2a ) we write ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})={\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ to mean $({\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}),0)$ , i.e., ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})$ does not act on the $\mathbf{A}$ -component. Second, we note that the $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ representation in ( 4.1 ) has a $0$ in the $\mathbf{A}$ -component; as such, in ( 4.2b ) we identify $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ with the pair $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{2})$ , which then matches the domain and range of the operators ${\mathcal{L}}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ . There are a few important advantages to the decomposition 24 24 24 This decomposition was first developed in [ 22 ] for stable blowup analysis of the 3D incompressible Euler equations. ( 4.1 ) and the definitions ( 4.2 ). First, the part $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ , which is used to capture unstable parts, is decoupled from the equation of $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ at the linear level, and so we can obtain decay estimates for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ directly using energy estimates and the dissipativity of $({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m},{\mathcal{L}}_{A})$ (see ( 3.13b ) and ( 3.30 )), without appealing to semigroups.
Second, we can obtain a representation formula (and an estimate) for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ by using Duhamel’s formula (4.3) $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}(s)=e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}+\int_{s_{\mathsf{in}}}^{s}e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s^{\prime})}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}$ for some initial data $\mathbf{V}_{2,in}$ , to be chosen later. We note that for ( 4.2 ) without the nonlinear parts, we can first obtain a decay estimate for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ , and then construct $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ using the above Duhamel formula, globally in time.
We obtain bounds for the full nonlinear system ( 4.2 ) by treating the nonlinear terms perturbatively. The Duhamel representation ( 4.3 ) for the unstable part of the perturbation is deceivingly simple; this is because we did not yet specify the initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}$ . In practice, we need to use the usual technique for constructing unstable manifolds, and apply the semigroup backward-in-time on the unstable piece of $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ . Additionally, in order to obtain initial data with compact support, we modify ( 4.3 ) as follows (4.4a) $\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}(s)$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{s}}}(s)-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s)+e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s_{\mathsf{in}})\bigl(1-\chi\bigl(\tfrac{y}{8R_{4}}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr),$ (4.4b) $\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{s}}}(s)$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{s_{\mathsf{in}}}^{s}e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s^{\prime})}\Pi_{\mathsf{s}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime},$ (4.4c) $\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s)$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-{\mathcal{L}}(s^{\prime}-s)}\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime},$ where $\chi$ is a smooth radial cutoff function with $\chi(y)=1$ for $|y|\leq 2/3$ , and $\chi(y)=0$ for $|y|\geq 1$ , $\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}$ is the orthogonal projection from ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ to ${\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ (see ( 3.45 )–( 3.46 )) and $\Pi_{\mathsf{s}}\triangleq{\rm Id}-\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}$ .
It is not difficult to see that ( 4.4 ) agrees with ( 4.3 ) for initial data taken as (4.4d) $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}=-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s_{\mathsf{in}})\chi\bigl(\tfrac{y}{8R_{4}}\bigr)=-\chi\bigl(\tfrac{y}{8R_{4}}\bigr)\int_{s_{\mathsf{in}}}^{\infty}e^{-{\mathcal{L}}(s^{\prime}-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}.$ Since the cutoff $\chi$ and elements of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ (the range of $\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ being a subset) are radially symmetric, if follows that so is $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}$ .
The detailed representation ( 4.4 ) shows that $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ is computed as a function of $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ ; for later purposes it is useful to codify this relation as a map, ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ , and to denote (4.5) ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\triangleq\mbox{Right Side of }\eqref{eq:V2_form2:a}.$ 4.2. Functional setting We introduce the following space for the perturbation $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})$ : (4.6) ${\mathcal{Z}}^{i}:={\mathcal{X}}^{i}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{i}\times{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{i}.$ Next, we introduce the spaces ${\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}$ and ${\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}$ , which are used for closing nonlinear estimates. Our goal is to perform both weighted $H^{2m}$ and weighted $H^{2m+2}$ estimates on ( 4.2 ), using the same compact operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ and the same projections $\Pi_{\mathsf{s}},\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}$ appearing in ( 4.2 ) and ( 4.4b )–( 4.4c ); that is, we do not wish to change ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ into ${\mathcal{K}}_{m+1}$ for the weighted $H^{2m+2}$ bound. Fix an arbitrary $m\geq m_{0}$ . For some $\mu_{m+1}>0$ to be chosen sufficiently small, using Theorem 3.2 , Proposition 3.4 (which in particular gives that ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}$ ), and the fact that by definition we have $\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}$ , we obtain $\displaystyle\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\mu_{m+1}\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}$ $\displaystyle\qquad\leq-\lambda\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\mu_{m+1}\bigl(\langle{\mathcal{L}}f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}+\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle\qquad\leq-\lambda\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\mu_{m+1}\bigl(-\lambda\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}^{2}+\bar{C}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}+C_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}\bigr)$ $\displaystyle\qquad\leq-\tfrac{9}{10}\lambda\bigl(\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\mu_{m+1}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}^{2}\bigr)+\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{10}\lambda+\mu_{m+1}(\bar{C}+\tfrac{5}{\lambda}C_{m}^{2})\bigr)\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2},$ for all $f\in\{(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}\}$ .
Choosing $\mu_{m+1}>0$ small enough in terms of $m$ and $\lambda$ , from the above bound we obtain the coercive estimate $\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\mu_{m+1}\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}\leq-\tfrac{9}{10}\lambda\bigl(\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}+\mu_{m+1}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}^{2}\bigr).$ Note that coercive estimates for ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}$ in both ${\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}$ are directly available from Theorem 3.2 . In light of the above coercive bounds, with $\mu_{m+1}>0$ chosen as above, we define the Hilbert spaces ${\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1},{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}$ according to the inner products (4.7a) $\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}\triangleq\mu_{m+1}\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}+\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},\qquad\|f\|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}^{2}=\langle f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}},$ (4.7b) $\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}\triangleq\mu_{m+1}\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}}+\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m}},\qquad\|f\|_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}^{2}=\langle f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}},$ and obtain that (4.8) $\langle({\mathcal{L}}-{\mathcal{K}}_{m})f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}\leq-\lambda_{1}\|f\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}},\qquad\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{A}f,f\rangle_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}\leq-\lambda_{1}\|f\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}},\qquad\lambda_{1}=\tfrac{9}{10}\lambda,$ for all $f\in\{(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}\}$ , respectively for all $f\in\{\mathbf{A}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\mathbf{A})\in{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}\}$ .
Estimate ( 4.8 ) shows that we can use the same compact operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ to simultaneously obtain coercive estimates in weighted $H^{2m+2}$ and weighted $H^{2m}$ spaces. 4.3. Nonlinear stability and the proof of Theorem 1.1 We recall from ( 3.47 ) (shifted so that the initial time is $s=s_{\mathsf{in}}$ instead of $s=0$ ) and ( 4.8 ), that the decay rates $\eta_{s},\eta$ , and $\lambda_{1}$ are given by (4.9) $(\eta_{s},\eta,\lambda_{1})=\bigl(\tfrac{3}{5},\tfrac{4}{5},\tfrac{9}{10}\bigr)\lambda,\qquad\eta_{s}<\eta<\lambda_{1}<\lambda.$ We also recall that the regularity parameter $m\geq m_{0}$ has been fixed.
The goal of this section is to prove: Theorem 4.1 ( Nonlinear stability). Fix $m\geq m_{0}$ .
There exists a sufficiently small $\delta>0$ such that
for any initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\mathbf{A}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ which is smooth enough 25 25 25 We require the ${\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ -regularity of $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}$ , a space which is stronger than ${\mathcal{Z}}^{m+1}$ , in order to obtain the local-in-time existence of a ${\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ -solution (see Footnote 27 ); in turn, this allows us to justify a few estimates, e.g. ( 4.8 ) for $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ which requires ${\mathcal{L}}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}$ . Note that this regularity requirement is only qualitative , and we only use Theorem 4.1 with an $C^{\infty}$ initial perturbation (see ( 4.12 )) in order to prove Theorem 1.1 .The only quantitative assumption on the initial data is given by ( 4.10 ). to ensure $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ (see ( 4.6 ))
and small enough to ensure (4.10) $\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}(s_{\mathsf{in}})\|_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}^{2}<\delta^{2},$ there exists
a global solution $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ to ( 4.2a ) with initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}$ , and a global solution $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ of ( 4.2b ) given by ( 4.4 ), satisfying the bounds (4.11a) $\displaystyle\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s))\|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}^{2}$ $\displaystyle<4\delta^{2}e^{-2\lambda_{1}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})},$ (4.11b) $\displaystyle\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2}(s),\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}(s))\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+2}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\delta_{Y}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})},\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad\delta_{Y}\triangleq\delta^{2/3},$ for all $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ .
We emphasize that we cannot prescribe the initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ ; rather, this data is constructed via ( 4.4d ) (simultaneously with the solution $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ ) to lie in a finite-dimensional subspace of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+2}$ . Remark 4.2 . The initial data for $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma,\mathbf{A})$ is obtained from Theorem 4.1 and the decomposition ( 4.1 ) at time $s=s_{\mathsf{in}}$ . In light of Theorem 4.1 , we identify the space $X_{2}$ mentioned in Remark 1.2 with an open ball in the weighted Sobolev space ${\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}$ defined in ( 4.7 ).
On the other hand, the space $X_{1}$ mentioned in Remark 1.2 consists of functions which are given as the sum of an element $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ which lies in open ball in the weighted Sobolev space ${\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}$ (see definition ( 4.7 )) and the element $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ constructed in ( 4.4d ), which lies in a finite-dimensional subspace of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+2}$ . Using the above Theorem 4.1 , we are now ready to prove Theorem 1.1 . We note though that Theorem 4.1 implies more than what was claimed in Theorem 1.1 ; these additional fine properties of the solution are discussed in Section 4.5 . Proof of Theorem 1.1 . We choose an initial perturbation $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}(y)=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}}),\mathbf{A}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}}))=(\tilde{U}_{1}(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})\mathbf{e}_{R},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}}),A(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})\mathbf{e}_{\theta})$ as in Theorem 4.1 , where $\xi=|y|$ . By definition, this initial data consists of radially-symmetric vectors/scalars.
Moreover, this initial perturbation may be chosen to ensure that $\tilde{U}_{1}(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}})+\bar{U}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}),\quad A(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}})\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}),\ \partial_{\xi}A(0,s_{\mathsf{in}})\neq 0,\quad\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}})+\bar{\Sigma}=1,\ \xi\geq C_{in},$ for some $C_{in}\geq 1$ large enough, to be chosen below. For example , we can choose (4.12a) $\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{1}(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})$ $\displaystyle=-\bar{U}(\xi)\Bigl(1-\chi\bigl(\tfrac{\xi}{C_{in}}\bigr)\Bigr),$ (4.12b) $\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})$ $\displaystyle=\bigl(1-\bar{\Sigma}(\xi)\bigr)\Bigl(1-\chi\bigl(\tfrac{\xi}{C_{in}}\bigr)\Bigr)$ (4.12c) $\displaystyle A(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})$ $\displaystyle=\tfrac{\xi}{C_{in}}\chi(\xi),$ where $C_{in}>0$ is to be determined, and $\chi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]$ is a radially symmetric and smooth cutoff function with $\chi(\xi)=1$ for $0\leq\xi\leq 1/2$ , and $\chi(\xi)=0$ for $\xi\geq 1$ . In order to verify that the data presented in ( 4.12 ) satisfies the assumption of Theorem 4.1 , we need to verify ( 4.10 ). 26 26 26 Indeed, initial data such as the one in ( 4.12 ) clearly satisfies the smoothness assumptions of Theorem 4.1 since the profile $(\bar{U},\bar{\Sigma})$ is $C^{\infty}$ smooth, radially-symmetric, and decays as $\xi\to\infty$ according to ( 2.4a ). Letting $F(y)=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ , we note from ( 2.4a ) that $|F(y)|\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{|y|\geq C_{in}/2}\langle y\rangle^{1-r}\in L^{2}(\varphi_{g})$ , where $\varphi_{g}$ is as defined in ( 3.15 ). Similarly, for $i\in\{m,m+1\}$ , from ( 2.4a ) we have $|\nabla^{2i}F|\lesssim_{i}\mathbf{1}_{|y|\geq C_{in}/2}\langle y\rangle^{1-2i-r}\in L^{2}(\varphi_{2i}^{2}\varphi_{g})$ , with $\varphi_{2i}$ as defined in ( 3.15 ). Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem we have that $\|F\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\to 0$ and $\|F\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}\to 0$ as $C_{in}\to\infty$ . In particular, we may ensure $\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\|_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}\leq\delta/2$ by choosing $C_{in}$ sufficiently large. Next, we note that the function $F(y)=\xi\chi(\xi)\mathbf{e}_{\theta}=C_{in}\mathbf{A}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}})$ , is $C^{\infty}$ smooth and has compact support in the unit ball. Moreover, using that the weight $\varphi_{A}$ defined in ( 3.28 ) satisfies $\varphi_{A}(\xi)\asymp\xi^{-\beta_{1}}$ with $\beta_{1}\in(3,4)$ , we deduce that $\|F\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}_{A}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{|\xi|\leq 1}\xi^{2}\xi^{-b_{1}}\xi d\xi\asymp 1$ . Thus, $\|F\|_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}\lesssim_{m}1$ , and hence $\|\mathbf{A}(s_{\mathsf{in}})\|_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}\leq\delta/2$ for $C_{in}$ sufficiently large. Thus, the initial data in ( 4.12 ) satisfies ( 4.10 ) whenever $C_{in}$ is sufficiently large. Without loss of generality, we may choose $C_{in}>16R_{4}$ , and thus definitions ( 4.12a )–( 4.12b ) imply that $\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\subset B(0,8R_{4})^{\complement}$ . Moreover, we have $\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}})+\bar{\mathbf{U}},-1+\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}})+\bar{\Sigma})\subset B(0,C_{in})$ and $\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{A}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\subset B(0,1)$ ; combined with $\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,in})=\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\subset B(0,8R_{4})$ (see ( 4.4d )), we deduce from ( 4.1 ) that the initial data $(\mathbf{U}(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}}),\Sigma(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}})-1,\mathbf{A}(\cdot,s_{\mathsf{in}}))$ to ( 2.3 ) has compact support in ${\mathbb{R}}^{2}$ . Additionally, the initial datum has no vacuum regions. Indeed, we have already shown that $\Sigma(y,s_{\mathsf{in}})=1$ for $|y|>8R_{4}$ . For $|y|\leq 8R_{4}$ , we use the fact that $\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}})=0$ , so that by ( 4.11 ) we obtain $\Sigma(y,s_{\mathsf{in}})=\bar{\Sigma}(y)+\tilde{\Sigma}_{2}(y,s_{\mathsf{in}})\geq\min_{|y|\leq 8R_{4}}\bar{\Sigma}(y)-C_{m}\delta_{Y}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\min_{|y|\leq 8R_{4}}\bar{\Sigma}(y)>0$ , since $\delta_{Y}=\delta^{2/3}$ was taken to be sufficiently small, and since $\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{m}\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}}$ for functions of compact support. Thus, the initial data for ( 2.3 ) is bounded away from vacuum. By construction (see ( 4.12c )), we have that $\partial_{\xi}A(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})|_{\xi=0}=C_{in}^{-1}>0$ . Moreover, since $\omega_{0}(R,t)|_{t=0}=\omega_{0}(R)=(R^{-1}+\partial_{R})u^{\theta}_{0}(R)$ , using the ansatz ( 2.1b ) we obtain $\omega_{0}(0)=\lim_{\xi\to 0^{+}}\tfrac{1}{rT}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\xi}+\partial_{\xi}\bigr)A(\xi,s_{\mathsf{in}})=\tfrac{2}{rT}\partial_{\xi}A(0,s_{\mathsf{in}})=\tfrac{2}{rTC_{in}}\neq 0.$ Moreover, note that for all $t\in[0,T)$ we have $\mathbf{u}(0,t)=\frac{1}{r}(T-t)^{\frac{1}{r}-1}(\mathbf{U}(0,s)+\mathbf{A}(0,s))=0$ , because $\mathbf{U}$ and $\mathbf{A}$ are smooth radially-symmetric vectors (hence, they vanish linearly in $\xi$ as $\xi\to 0^{+}$ , for all $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ ); thus the Lagrangian flow $X(a,t)$ emanating from $a=0$ is frozen at $0$ , i.e., $X(0,t)=0$ for all $t\in[0,T)$ . In light of the specific vorticity transport ( 1.2 ), the relation $\rho=(\alpha\sigma)^{1/\alpha}$ , and taking into account the self-similar transformation ( 2.1b ), we deduce $\omega(0,t)=\rho(0,t)\tfrac{\omega_{0}(0)}{\rho_{0}(0)}=\omega_{0}(0)\Bigl(\bigl(1-\tfrac{t}{T}\bigr)^{1/r-1}\tfrac{\Sigma(0,s)}{\Sigma(0,s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Bigr)^{1/\alpha}=\omega_{0}(0)\bigl(1-\tfrac{t}{T}\bigr)^{-(r-1)/(r\alpha)}\bigl(\tfrac{\bar{\Sigma}(0)+\tilde{\Sigma}(0,s)}{\bar{\Sigma}(0)+\tilde{\Sigma}(0,s_{\mathsf{in}})}\bigr)^{1/\alpha},$ for all $t\in[0,T)$ . Since the perturbation $\tilde{\Sigma}$ satisfies $\tilde{\Sigma}(0,s)\to 0$ as $s\to\infty$ (equivalently, as $t\to T$ ), exponentially fast in light of ( 4.11 ), and since $\omega_{0}(0)\neq 0$ , we obtain the desired vorticity blowup as $t\to T$ . In fact, the above identity also proves ( 1.8d ).
For a fixed $y\neq 0$ , the asymptotic convergence claimed in ( 1.8a )–( 1.8b ) follows from the ansatz ( 2.1b ) and the exponential decay of the perturbation $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}(\cdot,s),\tilde{\Sigma}(\cdot,s),\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(\cdot,s))$ as the self-similar time $s\to\infty$ (equivalently, as $t\to T$ ), established in ( 4.11 ). MM $\square$ 4.4. The proof of Theorem 4.1 The goal of this subsection is to prove Theorem 4.1 . Since the formula for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ (see ( 4.4c )) involves the future of the solution $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ , and since $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ enters the evolution ( 4.2a ) for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ through the nonlinear term, we cannot solve for the perturbation $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ directly. Instead, we reformulate ( 4.2a ) as a fixed point problem. We fix the initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}})=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})|_{s=s_{\mathsf{in}}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ ( 4.6 ) sufficiently smooth, and sufficiently small such that ( 4.10 ) holds.
We define the spaces $Y_{1}$ and $Y_{2}$ , which capture the decay of the solutions in time (4.13a) $\displaystyle\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})\|_{Y_{1}}$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}}e^{\lambda_{1}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\bigl(\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s))\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{W}}^{m+1}}+\|\mathbf{A}(s)\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{m+1}}\bigr)^{1/2},$ (4.13b) $\displaystyle\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})\|_{Y_{2}}$ $\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}}e^{\lambda_{1}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\bigl(\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s))\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\|\mathbf{A}(s)\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{A}}\bigr)^{1/2}.$ Showing $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})\in Y_{i}$ for $i\in\{1,2\}$ implies that suitable norms of $\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s),\mathbf{A}(s)$ decay with a rate $e^{-\lambda_{1}s}$ as $s\to\infty$ ; recall cf. ( 4.9 ) that $\lambda_{1}=\frac{9}{10}\lambda$ . During the proof we sometimes abuse notation to write (4.14) $\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{2}}=\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},0)\|_{Y_{2}},$ i.e., when the $\mathbf{A}$ -component is irrelevant for an estimate. Next, we define an operator ${\mathcal{T}}$ (see ( 4.17 )), whose fixed point (see ( 4.18 )) is the desired solution of ( 4.2a ). We remark that throughout the remainder of this proof, we distinguish the $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ -components of an input of a map (e.g. ${\mathcal{T}}$ , or ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ ) by variables with a “hat” (e.g. $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})$ ), and the output of these maps by variables with a “tilde” (e.g. $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ ). With this notational convention in place, the two-step process is: • first, for $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\in Y_{2}$ , we define (4.15) $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}={\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}),$ where the linear map ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ is defined by ( 4.5 ), via ( 4.4 ); • second, we define $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})$ as the solution 27 27 27 To construct the solution of ( 4.16 ) locally in time, we use an iterative scheme, and arguments similar to those in Section 3.4.2 .
For a fixed $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}={\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\hat{\Sigma}_{1})$ , we first construct a $C_{c}^{\infty}$ solution to an $\varepsilon$ -regularized version of ( 4.16 ), in which the operator ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\cdot)$ and the term $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ are replaced by $C^{\infty}_{c}$ approximations ${\mathcal{K}}_{m,\varepsilon}(\cdot)$ and $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,\varepsilon}$ . We start with initial data $G_{0}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,\varepsilon}|_{s=s_{\mathsf{in}}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}$ and iteratively for $n\geq 0$ construct $G_{n+1}$ by solving $\partial_{s}G_{n+1}={\mathcal{L}}_{V}(G_{n+1})-{\mathcal{K}}_{m,\varepsilon}(G_{n+1})+\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(G_{n}+\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,\varepsilon},G_{n+1}+\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,\varepsilon}),\qquad G_{n+1}|_{s=s_{\mathsf{in}}}=G_{0},$ where we rewrite the quadratic nonlinearity ${\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\cdot)$ from ( 2.7 ) as the bilinear form $\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\cdot,\cdot)$ with the derivative acting on the second entry (e.g. $\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{U}((\mathbf{U},\Sigma,\mathbf{A}),(\mathbf{U}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}))=-\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}^{\prime}-\alpha\Sigma\nabla\Sigma^{\prime}-\mathbf{A}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{A}^{\prime}$ ). Clearly $\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}},\tilde{\mathbf{V}})={\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}})$ .
The above equation is a linear symmetric hyperbolic system for $G_{n+1}$ , which can be solved following the arguments in Section 3.4.2 .
By proving convergence as $n\to\infty$ of the Picard iterates $\{G_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}$ , and afterwards taking the regularization parameter $\varepsilon\to 0$ , similarly to Section 3.4.2 we obtain the local existence of a $L^{\infty}_{s}({\mathcal{Z}}^{m+1})$ solution, where we recall cf. ( 4.16 ) that ${\mathcal{Z}}^{i}={\mathcal{X}}^{i}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{i}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{i}_{A}$ . We emphasize that for initial data $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1,in}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ , e.g. for the initial data from Theorem 4.1 , since ${\mathcal{K}}(\cdot):{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ (item (c) in Proposition 3.4 and Lemma 4.6 ), the above described construction procedure via $C^{\infty}_{c}$ approximations also gives the local existence of a solution with this higher ${\mathcal{Z}}^{m+2}$ regularity, so that locally in time ${\mathcal{L}}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}$ and ${\mathcal{L}}_{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}})\in{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{m+1}$ . of a modified version of ( 4.2a ), namely (4.16a) $\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}$ $\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})-{\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})+{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})),$ (4.16b) $\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}|_{s=s_{\mathsf{in}}}$ $\displaystyle=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}(s_{\mathsf{in}}),\mathbf{A}(s_{\mathsf{in}}))\quad\mbox{as in Theorem~\ref{thm:non}},$ where we recall the notation ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1})=({\mathcal{K}}_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}),0)$ , and $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})=((\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})+{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}),\mathbf{A})$ ; that is, neither ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ nor ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ act on the $\mathbf{A}$ -component. Concatenating the two steps given above defines a map which takes as input $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})$ and outputs the solution of ( 4.16 ): (4.17) $\bigl((\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}),\mathbf{A}\bigr)=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:non_fix}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{T}}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})=\bigl({\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}),{\mathcal{T}}_{A}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\bigr).$ Denoting by ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}$ the restriction of ${\mathcal{T}}$ to the $(\mathbf{U},\Sigma)$ -components, we have thus reformulated the system ( 4.2 )
as a fixed point problem : find $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ such that (4.18) $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})={\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1}),$ with $\mathbf{A}$ and $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{2})$ computed as ${\mathcal{T}}_{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ and ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ , respectively. The proof of Theorem 4.1 reduces to establishing that the operator ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}$ is a contraction with respect to the norm in ( 4.14 ), in a small vicinity of the zero state, characterized by the smallness parameters (4.19) $\delta_{Y}=\delta^{2/3},\qquad\delta\ll_{m}1,$ as in the statement of Theorem 4.1 .
The proof of Theorem 4.1 is broken down in two steps, according to Proposition 4.3 (which shows that the map ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}$ maps the ball of radius $\delta_{Y}$ in $Y_{2}$ into itself), and Proposition 4.4 (which shows that ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}$ is a contraction for the topology $Y_{2}$ ). Proposition 4.3 . There exists a positive $\delta_{0}\ll_{m}1$ such that for any $\delta<\delta_{0}$ and any $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\in Y_{2}$ with $\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{2}}<\delta_{Y}$ , we have $\|{\mathcal{T}}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{1}}<2\delta,\qquad\|{\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{2}}<\delta_{Y},\qquad\|{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}\lesssim_{m}\delta_{Y}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})},$ for all $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ . Proposition 4.4 . There exists a positive $\delta_{0}\ll_{m}1$ such that for any $\delta<\delta_{0}$ and any pairs $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,a},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,a})$ , $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,b},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,b})\in Y_{2}$ with $\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,a},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,a})\|_{Y_{2}}<\delta_{Y}$ and $\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,b},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,b})\|_{Y_{2}}<\delta_{Y}$ , we have $\|{\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,a},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,a})-{\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,b},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,b})\|_{Y_{2}}<\tfrac{1}{2}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,a},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,a})-(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,b},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,b})\|_{Y_{2}}.$ From Proposition 4.3 and Proposition 4.4 we directly obtain: Proof of Theorem 4.1 . Propositions 4.3 and 4.4 allow us to apply a Banach fix-point theorem for the operator ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}$ , in the ball of radius $\delta_{Y}$ around the origin in $Y_{2}$ ; this results in a unique fixed point $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ in this ball, as claimed in ( 4.18 ). Upon defining $\mathbf{A}\triangleq{\mathcal{T}}_{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ and $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{2})\triangleq{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ , by construction we have that $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{A})$ solves ( 4.2a ) and $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}=(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{2},0)$ solves ( 4.2b ). In view of the definition of the $Y_{1}$ norm in ( 4.13a ) and the bound $\|{\mathcal{T}}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{1}}<2\delta$ (which follows from the first estimate in Proposition 4.3 ), we deduce that ( 4.11a ) holds. Similarly, the third estimate in Proposition 4.3 (applied to $(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})$ ) yields ( 4.11b ), thereby concluding the proof of Theorem 4.1 . MM $\square$ The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of Propositions 4.3 and 4.4 .
In subsection 4.4.1 , we obtain suitable estimates for the linear map ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ ; in particular, in Lemma 4.5 we demonstrate a smoothing effect for $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ , which allows us to overcome the loss of a space derivative due to the term $\nabla\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2}$ , present in the first equation of ( 4.2 ). In subsection 4.4.2 we prove Proposition 4.3 , while in subsection 4.4.3 , we prove Proposition 4.4 ; here we emphasize that due to the transport term $\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}$ , estimating the difference ${\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,a},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,a})-{\mathcal{T}}_{U,\Sigma}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1,b},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1,b})$ is made possible because in ( 4.13 ) we have chosen $Y_{2}$ (modeled on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{A}$ ) to be less regular than $Y_{1}$ (modeled on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}$ and ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+1}_{A}$ ) . 4.4.1. Estimates on ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ Recall the decomposition ( 3.45 ) of ${\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ into stable and unstable modes. In light of definitions ( 4.4b ) and ( 4.4c ), we establish the following decay and smoothing estimates for the stable and unstable parts of ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}$ : Lemma 4.5 . For $f\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , we have $\displaystyle\|e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{s}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},$ $\displaystyle\|e^{-{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}e^{\eta(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},$ for all $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ , where $\eta$ and $\eta_{s}$ are as in ( 4.9 ). Proof. Denote $F(s)=e^{{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{s}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f$ . Since $\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}$ projects onto the finite-dimensional space ${\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ , defined in ( 3.46 ), which is spanned by ${\mathcal{X}}^{\infty}$ smooth functions (see Section 3.6 ), we have the equivalence (4.20) $\|\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{n}}\asymp_{n,m}\|\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}g\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},\qquad\forall g\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m},\ n\geq m.$ Using ( 4.20 ), the fact that $\Pi_{\mathsf{s}}={\rm Id}-\Pi_{\mathsf{u}},\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ , and the smoothing property ${\mathcal{K}}_{m}:{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\to{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{m}$ (which follows from item (c) in Proposition 3.4 ), we obtain $\displaystyle\|F(s_{\mathsf{in}})\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}+\|\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\|\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}+\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim_{m}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ Using the decay property ( 3.47 ) of the semigroup and the fact that $F(s_{\mathsf{in}})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{m}_{\mathsf{s}}$ , we deduce $\|F(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq C_{m}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|F(s_{\mathsf{in}})\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\leq C_{m}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}.$ Combining the above bound with the energy estimates on ${\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}$ , which follow from Theorem 3.2 (more specifically, ( 3.13a )), we obtain $\quad\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{ds}\|F(s)\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}\leq-\lambda\|F(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}^{2}+\bar{C}\|F(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{0}}^{2}\leq-\lambda\|F(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}^{2}+\bar{C}C_{m}^{2}e^{-2\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}^{2}.$ Since $\eta_{s}=\frac{3}{5}\lambda<\lambda$ , integrating the above estimate in time we deduce the first inequality of Lemma 4.5 . From Proposition 3.7 we have that ${\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{u}}^{m}$ is ${\mathcal{L}}$ -invariant and ${\mathcal{L}}|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{u}^{m}}$ is bounded.
Using ( 4.20 ) and ( 3.48 ), we establish $\|e^{-{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}\lesssim_{m}\|e^{-{\mathcal{L}}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\Pi_{\mathsf{u}}{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim_{m}e^{\eta(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|{\mathcal{K}}_{m}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}\lesssim e^{\eta(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}},$ which is the second inequality claimed in Lemma 4.5 . MM $\square$ Using Lemma 4.5 and the fact that ${\mathcal{L}}$ generates a semigroup, we obtain a direct estimate for the operator ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}$ , as defined in ( 4.4 ). We recall from ( 4.13b ) and ( 4.14 ) the definition of the norm $Y_{2}$ . Lemma 4.6 . For $(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\in Y_{2}$ , for all $s\geq s_{\mathsf{in}}$ we have $\|{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}\lesssim_{m}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{2}}.$ Proof. Denote $M=\|(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\tilde{\Sigma}_{1})\|_{Y_{2}}$ , and recall from ( 4.4a ) that ${\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\widehat{\Sigma}_{1})(s)$ is given by the sum of three terms. We also recall from ( 4.9 ) that $\eta_{s}<\eta<\lambda_{1}$ .
Applying Lemma 4.5 to $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{s}}}$ (as defined in ( 4.4b )), and to $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}$ (as defined in ( 4.4c )), we obtain $\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{s}}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\int_{s_{\mathsf{in}}}^{s}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s^{\prime})}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s^{\prime}),\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}(s^{\prime}))\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}ds^{\prime}$ (4.21a) $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}M\int_{s_{\mathsf{in}}}^{s}e^{-\eta_{s}(s-s^{\prime})}e^{-\lambda_{1}(s^{\prime}-s_{\mathsf{in}})}ds^{\prime}\lesssim_{m}Me^{-\eta_{s}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})},$ $\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}$ $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{\eta(s^{\prime}-s)}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{1}(s^{\prime}),\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}(s^{\prime}))\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m}}ds^{\prime}$ (4.21b) $\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}M\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{\eta(s^{\prime}-s)}e^{-\lambda_{1}(s^{\prime}-s_{\mathsf{in}})}ds^{\prime}\lesssim_{m}Me^{-\lambda_{1}(s-s_{\mathsf{in}})}.$ The second estimates in particular implies $\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s_{\mathsf{in}})\|_{{\mathcal{X}}^{m+3}}\lesssim_{m}M$ . Denote $g=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{2,{\mathsf{u}}}(s_{\mathsf{in}})(1-\chi(\frac{y}{8R_{4}}))$ , corresponding to the third term on the right side of ( 4.4a ). By definition $\mathrm{supp}(g)\cap B(0,5R_{4})=\emptyset$ .
